13.3 Envelopes and model potentials

Definitions, results, and proofs on this subject.

16 Envelopes and model potentials

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

Throughout this chapter $X$ is a connected compact Kähler manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$ with a fixed Kähler form $\omega $, and $\theta $ is a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form on $X$, as in the two preceding chapters. Many potentials are defined as envelopes: given an obstacle $f$, one takes the largest $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function that lies below $f$ outside a pluripolar set. For instance, the extremal potential $V_\theta $ of definition 14.2.6 is the envelope of the obstacle $0$. Two features of the global setting require care. The obstacle is often only a Borel function, so we must keep track of which inequalities hold everywhere and which only quasi-everywhere. And there may be no $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function below the obstacle at all: two $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions need not have a common $\theta $-plurisubharmonic minorant (example 16.1.7).

The basic property of an envelope is that its non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure is carried by the contact set, where the envelope touches the obstacle; for quasi-continuous obstacles in a big class this is theorem 16.2.5. Away from the contact set, the envelope of a lower semicontinuous obstacle can be replaced locally by a maximal plurisubharmonic function, so its measure vanishes there, and quasi-continuity lets us approximate a general obstacle by lower semicontinuous ones. Regularity of the obstacles also passes to the envelope. If finitely many obstacles have bounded Laplacian with respect to the Riemannian metric of $\omega $, then so does the envelope of their minimum. For a Kähler background we prove this with an exponentially penalized Monge–Ampère equation (theorem 16.2.24), which also shows that envelopes of finite minima of $C^{1,1}$ obstacles are $C^{1,1}$ (theorem 16.2.26); in a big class the bound holds locally on the ample locus, the complement of the non-Kähler locus (theorem 16.2.29). On the contact set of such an obstacle with an arbitrary potential lying below it, the non-pluripolar measure of the potential is then computed from the second derivatives of the obstacle (theorem 16.2.31).

The second half of the chapter studies envelopes that prescribe singularities rather than values. The singularity envelope $P_\theta [u]$ of a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function $u$ is the upper envelope of all nonpositive $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions $v$ with $v\leq u+C$ for some constant $C$, which may depend on $v$. It has the same non-pluripolar masses as $u$ but may be less singular, and its fixed points of positive mass are called model potentials. The main tool is extrapolation (theorem 16.4.4): if $u$ is more singular than $v$ and has almost the same mass, then for some $a>1$ the function $v+a(u-v)$, which continues the segment from $v$ to $u$ beyond $u$, still has a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic minorant of positive mass. From this we derive the domination principle for potentials of full mass relative to a reference potential (theorem 16.4.6) and the characterization of $P_\theta [u]$, for $u$ of positive mass, as the largest nonpositive potential that is less singular than $u$ and has the same mass (theorem 16.4.7). Rooftop envelopes satisfy a mass inequality (theorem 16.5.5), and upper envelopes preserve the comparison of singularities through multiplier ideals (section 16.6).

Finally we introduce the Monge–Ampère capacity $\operatorname {Cap}_{\theta ,\phi }$ relative to a reference potential $\phi $, prove that it is comparable with the capacity of a Kähler form when $\phi $ has positive mass (theorem 16.9.8), and use it to measure the deep sublevel sets $\{ u<\phi -s\} $ of potentials of full mass relative to $\phi $. These estimates are what the next chapter needs in order to integrate by parts with singular potentials. Singularity envelopes of potentials of zero mass, and contact concentration in arbitrary pseudo-effective classes, depend on the Monge–Ampère equations solved there (section 17.5, section 17.4.4). Historical remarks and references for this chapter are collected in section B.16.

16.1 Obstacles and rooftops

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

The fixed Kähler form $\omega $ serves as a reference: volumes, norms and the Laplacians of obstacles are taken with respect to $\omega $, also when an envelope is formed with respect to a different background. We call the elements of $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ (definition 13.1.3) potentials; they are pointwise defined functions, not identically $-\infty $. Recall from definition 14.2.6 that $u$ is more singular than $v$, written $u\preceq v$, if $u\leq v+C$ for some constant $C$. We write $u\sim v$, and say that $u$ and $v$ have the same singularity type, if $u\preceq v$ and $v\preceq u$.

Two facts are used constantly. First, pluripolar sets have Lebesgue measure zero (see the remarks after definition 10.1.1). Second, if two quasi-plurisubharmonic functions satisfy $u\leq v$ almost everywhere, then $u\leq v$ everywhere, by lemma 13.1.4(1). Consequently, an inequality between quasi-plurisubharmonic functions that holds quasi-everywhere, that is, outside a pluripolar set (definition 10.1.1), holds everywhere. In particular, if $u\sim v$, then $u$ and $v$ have the same pole set and $u-v$ is bounded outside it.

Quasi-continuity on $X$ is the notion of definition 9.4.1, read in local charts as in section 15.6: a Borel function $f\colon X\to [-\infty ,+\infty ]$ is quasi-continuous if its restriction to every coordinate chart, identified with a bounded open set $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$, is quasi-continuous for the Monge–Ampère capacity $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ of definition 9.3.2. The following lemma shows that it suffices to check this on the charts of one atlas; in proposition 16.9.9 we show that quasi-continuous functions are also continuous outside open sets of small global capacity.

Lemma 16.1.1. Let $D$ and $D'$ be coordinate charts of $X$, identified with bounded open subsets of $\mathbb C^ n$.

  1. For every compact set $L\subset D\cap D'$ there is a constant $c$ such that $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)\leq c\operatorname {Cap}_{D'}(E)$ for every Borel set $E\subset L$.

  2. If $E\subset X$ is pluripolar, then $\operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(E\cap D)=0$.

  3. If a Borel function $f\colon X\to [-\infty ,+\infty ]$ is quasi-continuous on each chart of an atlas of $X$, then it is quasi-continuous on every coordinate chart.

Proof. Assertions (1) and (3) are contained in lemma 15.6.1: (1) is its inequality for Borel sets, applied with the roles of $D$ and $D'$ exchanged, and (3) is its final assertion.

For (2) we first observe that if $W\subset D$ is open, then every competitor $v$ for $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ restricts to a plurisubharmonic function on $W$ with values in $[0,1]$, and the restriction of the Bedford–Taylor measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$ to $W$ is the Bedford–Taylor measure of this restriction. Hence $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)\leq \operatorname {Cap}_ W(E)$ for every Borel set $E\subset W$, and, taking infima over the open sets $G$ with $A\subset G\subset W$, also $\operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(A)\leq \operatorname {Cap}_ W^*(A)$ for every set $A\subset W$.

Now cover $D$ by countably many open balls $B_ k$, in the coordinates of $D$, with $\overline{B_ k}\subset D$. Since the condition in definition 10.1.1 is local, $E\cap B_ k$ is pluripolar in $B_ k$, so $\operatorname {Cap}_{B_ k}^*(E\cap B_ k)=0$ by theorem 10.1.4. By the observation above, $\operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(E\cap B_ k)=0$, and the countable subadditivity of $\operatorname {Cap}_ D^*$ (proposition 9.3.3) gives $\operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(E\cap D)=0$. $\square$

Quasi-plurisubharmonic functions are quasi-continuous, and quasi-continuity is preserved by the following operations.

Lemma 16.1.2. Let $\theta '$ be a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form on $X$.

  1. Every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ')$ is quasi-continuous. In particular, every potential $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is quasi-continuous.

  2. Finite maxima and minima of quasi-continuous functions on $X$ are quasi-continuous.

  3. If $f$ is quasi-continuous on $X$ and $g\colon X\to [-\infty ,+\infty ]$ is a Borel function that agrees with $f$ outside a pluripolar set, then $g$ is quasi-continuous.

Proof. (1) By lemma 7.3.1 every point of $X$ has a neighborhood on which $\theta '=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$ for a smooth function $h$, so $X$ is covered by finitely many coordinate balls $D_ i$ on which $\theta '=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ i$ with $h_ i$ smooth near $\overline{D_ i}$. On $D_ i$ the function $u+h_ i$ is plurisubharmonic by lemma 13.1.4(2), hence quasi-continuous by theorem 9.4.2, and so is $u=(u+h_ i)-h_ i$, because $h_ i$ is continuous and real. By lemma 16.1.1(3), $u$ is quasi-continuous on every chart.

(2) Let $f_1,\ldots ,f_ k$ be quasi-continuous, let $K$ be a compact subset of a chart $D$, and let $\varepsilon >0$. Choose open sets $G_ i\subset D$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_ i)<\varepsilon /k$ such that $f_ i$ is continuous on $K\setminus G_ i$. By subadditivity, $G=G_1\cup \cdots \cup G_ k$ has capacity less than $\varepsilon $, and all the $f_ i$, hence also their maximum and minimum, are continuous on $K\setminus G$.

(3) Let $D$ be a chart, $K\subset D$ compact and $\varepsilon >0$. The set $\{ f\ne g\} $ is pluripolar, so by lemma 16.1.1(2) its intersection with $D$ is contained in an open set $G'\subset D$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G')<\varepsilon /2$. If $G\subset D$ is an open set with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\varepsilon /2$ such that $f$ is continuous on $K\setminus G$, then $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G\cup G')<\varepsilon $, and $g=f$ is continuous on $K\setminus (G\cup G')$. $\square$

Definition 16.1.3. Let $f\colon X\to [-\infty ,+\infty ]$ be a Borel function, called an obstacle, and put

\[ \mathcal A_\theta (f)=\{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):v\leq f \text{ quasi-everywhere}\} . \]

The members of $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$ are called the competitors for $f$. If $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$ is nonempty and uniformly bounded above, the obstacle envelope of $f$ is

\[ P_\theta (f)=\bigl (\sup \mathcal A_\theta (f)\bigr )^*, \]

where ${}^*$ denotes the upper semicontinuous regularization. If $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$ is empty, we put $P_\theta (f)=-\infty $. If $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$ is nonempty but not uniformly bounded above, $P_\theta (f)$ is not defined, and whenever we use $P_\theta (f)$ for an obstacle that is not bounded above, we verify the upper bound first. If $f\leq b$ quasi-everywhere for a constant $b$, the upper bound is automatic: every competitor satisfies $v\leq b$ quasi-everywhere, hence almost everywhere, and therefore everywhere by lemma 13.1.4(1).

As for the capacity $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ (definition 9.3.2) and the relative extremal functions of chapter 10, a competitor is a member of the family whose supremum defines the object under consideration; we always say which obstacle, envelope or capacity is meant.

Proposition 16.1.4. Let $f$ be a Borel obstacle.

  1. If $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$ is nonempty and uniformly bounded above, then $P_\theta (f)\in \mathcal A_\theta (f)$; thus $P_\theta (f)$ is the largest competitor for $f$.

  2. If $f$ is upper semicontinuous, then every competitor $v$ for $f$ satisfies $v\leq f$ everywhere.

  3. The envelope does not change when $f$ is modified on a pluripolar set, and $P_\theta (f+c)=P_\theta (f)+c$ for $c\in \mathbb R$. If $g$ is a Borel obstacle with $f\leq g$ quasi-everywhere and $\mathcal A_\theta (g)$ is uniformly bounded above, then $P_\theta (f)\leq P_\theta (g)$.

  4. Suppose that $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$ is nonempty and uniformly bounded above, and let $g$ be a Borel obstacle with $f-c\leq g\leq f+c$ quasi-everywhere for a constant $c\geq 0$. Then $\mathcal A_\theta (g)$ is nonempty and uniformly bounded above, and

    \[ P_\theta (f)-c\leq P_\theta (g)\leq P_\theta (f)+c. \]

    In particular the two envelopes have the same pole set and differ by at most $c$ outside it.

  5. Let Borel obstacles $f_ j$ decrease pointwise to $f$, and suppose that $\mathcal A_\theta (f_1)$ is uniformly bounded above. Then $P_\theta (f_ j)$ decreases pointwise to $P_\theta (f)$. Here the families may be empty; in particular, $P_\theta (f_ j)$ decreases to the constant $-\infty $ if and only if $\mathcal A_\theta (f)=\varnothing $.

Proof. (1) Cover $X$ by finitely many coordinate balls on which $\theta $ has smooth local potentials $h$ (lemma 7.3.1). Applying theorem 8.3.2 on each ball to the plurisubharmonic functions $v+h$, $v\in \mathcal A_\theta (f)$, and taking the union of the resulting countable subfamilies, we find competitors $v_1,v_2,\ldots $ with $(\sup _ kv_ k)^*=P_\theta (f)$. The finite maxima $w_ k=\max \{ v_1,\ldots ,v_ k\} $ are again competitors, and they increase with $k$. Each $w_ k$ satisfies $w_ k\leq f$ outside a pluripolar set $N_ k$, and $N=\bigcup _ kN_ k$ is pluripolar by theorem 10.1.4. By corollary 10.2.2, $\sup _ kw_ k=P_\theta (f)$ outside a pluripolar set $N'$. Hence $P_\theta (f)\leq f$ outside $N\cup N'$. Since the family is nonempty and uniformly bounded above, $P_\theta (f)$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic by theorem 8.3.2. Thus $P_\theta (f)$ is a competitor, and by construction it dominates every competitor.

(2) Let $v$ be a competitor, let $x\in X$, and choose a coordinate ball $B(x,r)$ and a smooth function $h$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\theta $ near its closure. Since $v+h$ is plurisubharmonic and $v\leq f$ outside a Lebesgue null set, the sub-mean value inequality gives

\[ v(x)+h(x)\leq \frac1{|B(x,r)|}\int _{B(x,r)}(v+h)\, \mathrm dV_{2n} \leq \sup _{B(x,r)}(f+h). \]

As $r\to 0$, the right side tends to at most $f(x)+h(x)$, because $f+h$ is upper semicontinuous. Hence $v(x)\leq f(x)$.

(3) A modification of $f$ on a pluripolar set does not change $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$, since the union of two pluripolar sets is pluripolar. Moreover $\mathcal A_\theta (f+c)=\mathcal A_\theta (f)+c$, and $\mathcal A_\theta (f)\subset \mathcal A_\theta (g)$ if $f\leq g$ quasi-everywhere. These relations between the families of competitors give the three assertions.

(4) By (3), $\mathcal A_\theta (f)-c\subset \mathcal A_\theta (g)\subset \mathcal A_\theta (f)+c$. Hence $\mathcal A_\theta (g)$ is nonempty and uniformly bounded above, and the two inequalities follow from (3).

(5) By (3) the envelopes $P_\theta (f_ j)$ decrease; if some family $\mathcal A_\theta (f_ j)$ is empty, then so are all later ones and $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$. Let $v$ be the pointwise limit of $P_\theta (f_ j)$. Suppose first that $v(x_0)>-\infty $ for some $x_0$. Then $\sup _ XP_\theta (f_ j)\geq v(x_0)$ for every $j$, so $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ by corollary 14.2.5. By (1), $v\leq P_\theta (f_ j)\leq f_ j$ outside a pluripolar set $N_ j$, so $v\leq \inf _ jf_ j=f$ outside the pluripolar set $\bigcup _ jN_ j$. Thus $v$ is a competitor for $f$ and $v\leq P_\theta (f)$; the reverse inequality follows from $P_\theta (f)\leq P_\theta (f_ j)$. Suppose next that $v\equiv -\infty $. A competitor $w$ for $f$ would be a competitor for every $f_ j$, so $w\leq P_\theta (f_ j)$ for all $j$ and $w\leq v$, which is impossible. Hence $\mathcal A_\theta (f)=\varnothing $ and $P_\theta (f)=-\infty =v$. $\square$

Definition 16.1.5. For $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, the rooftop envelope of $u$ and $v$ is

\[ P_\theta (u,v)=P_\theta (\min \{ u,v\} ). \]

We also put $P_\theta (u,-\infty )=P_\theta (-\infty ,u)=-\infty $ for $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cup \{ -\infty \} $.

The obstacle $\min \{ u,v\} $ is upper semicontinuous and bounded above. By proposition 16.1.4, a potential is therefore a competitor for it exactly when it lies below both $u$ and $v$ everywhere. Thus $P_\theta (u,v)$ is the largest common minorant of $u$ and $v$ in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ if they have one, and $P_\theta (u,v)=-\infty $ if they have none.

Proposition 16.1.6. The rooftop envelope is commutative, associative, increasing in both arguments and idempotent, that is, $P_\theta (u,u)=u$. For a constant $c$, $P_\theta (u+c,v+c)=P_\theta (u,v)+c$. If $u_ j,v_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ decrease pointwise to $u$ and $v$, where the limits may be identically $-\infty $, then $P_\theta (u_ j,v_ j)$ decreases pointwise to $P_\theta (u,v)$. Finally, for $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and every constant $C\in \mathbb R$, the rooftop envelope $P_\theta (u+C,V_\theta )$ is a potential with $P_\theta (u+C,V_\theta )\sim u$.

Proof. Commutativity, monotonicity and idempotence follow directly from the description of $P_\theta (u,v)$ as the largest common minorant. For associativity, let also $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cup \{ -\infty \} $. A potential lies below $P_\theta (u,v)$ and $w$ if and only if it lies below $u$, $v$ and $w$: when $P_\theta (u,v)\ne -\infty $ this is the maximality of $P_\theta (u,v)$, and otherwise neither condition can be satisfied. Hence $P_\theta (P_\theta (u,v),w)$ and $P_\theta (u,P_\theta (v,w))$ are both the largest common minorant of $u$, $v$ and $w$, or both equal $-\infty $ if there is none. The behavior under constant shifts and decreasing limits follows from proposition 16.1.4, applied to the obstacles $\min \{ u_ j,v_ j\} $, which are bounded above by $\sup _ Xu_1$ and decrease to $\min \{ u,v\} $. If $u$ or $v$ is identically $-\infty $, this limit obstacle has no competitor, in accordance with our convention.

For the last assertion put $B=\max \{ \sup _ Xu,-C\} $. Then $u-B\leq 0$, so $u-B\leq V_\theta $, and also $u-B\leq u+C$. Hence $u-B$ is a common minorant of $u+C$ and $V_\theta $, and

\[ u-B\leq P_\theta (u+C,V_\theta )\leq u+C. \]
$\square$

Example 16.1.7. On $\mathbb P^1$ let $\theta =\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (1+|z|^2)$ be the Fubini–Study form of example 14.5.6, in the affine coordinate $z$, so that $\int _{\mathbb P^1}\theta =1$. Put

\[ u_0=\log \frac{|z|^2}{1+|z|^2},\qquad u_\infty =-\log (1+|z|^2). \]

In the coordinate $\zeta =1/z$ near $\infty $ we have $\log (1+|z|^2)=\log (1+|\zeta |^2)-\log |\zeta |^2$, so $\theta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (1+|\zeta |^2)$ there, while $u_0=-\log (1+|\zeta |^2)$ and $u_\infty =\log (|\zeta |^2/(1+|\zeta |^2))$. Thus the substitution $z\leftrightarrow \zeta $ exchanges the two functions. On $\mathbb C$ we have $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |z|^2=[0]$ by the Poincaré–Lelong formula (theorem 7.4.5), and $u_0$ is smooth near $\infty $ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0=0$ there. Hence $u_0,u_\infty \in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^1,\theta )$, with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0=[0]$ and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\infty =[\infty ]$, the currents of integration at the two points.

Suppose that $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^1,\theta )$ is a common minorant of $u_0$ and $u_\infty $. Near $0$ the function $u_0$ is $\log |z|^2$ plus a smooth function, so $\nu (u_0,0)=1$ by proposition 12.1.4, and $w\leq u_0$ gives $\nu (w,0)\geq 1$ by the description of Lelong numbers through logarithmic growth in proposition 12.1.1; likewise $\nu (w,\infty )\geq 1$. In dimension one, the Lelong number of a subharmonic function $s$ at a point is the mass that the measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s$ gives to that point (theorem 12.1.3). Hence the positive measure $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ has atoms of mass at least one at $0$ and at $\infty $, whereas its total mass is $\int _{\mathbb P^1}\theta =1$, because $\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w,1\rangle =\langle w,\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}1\rangle =0$. This contradiction shows that $P_\theta (u_0,u_\infty )=-\infty $, although $u_0$ and $u_\infty $ are both $\theta $-plurisubharmonic.

16.2 Measures on contact sets

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

For $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ we write $\theta _ u=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ and denote by $\mu _ u=\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle $ the non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure of $u$, as in proposition 15.8.5; its total mass $\mu _ u(X)=\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle $ is called the mass of $u$. When a different background is used, we say so or indicate it by a subscript. A contact set is a set $\{ u=f\} $ on which two functions agree; for Borel functions it is a Borel set, but it need not be closed (for instance, if $U\subset X$ is open, the functions $0$ and $\mathbf1_{X\setminus U}$ agree exactly on $U$).

Three properties of these measures are used throughout. They give no mass to pluripolar sets (lemma 15.1.2), in particular to pole sets, so points where a potential equals $-\infty $ can be ignored in measure-theoretic statements. They are local in the plurifine topology (proposition 15.1.5): if two potentials agree on a plurifine open set $O$, their measures agree on $O$; and for potentials $u,v$ and $c\in \mathbb R$ the set $\{ u>v+c\} $ is plurifine open by proposition 10.5.1. Finally, multilinearity and positivity give

\[ \mu _{(1-t)u+tv}\geq t^ n\mu _ v \qquad (u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ),\ 0\leq t\leq 1), \]

and the mass is monotone: $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle \leq \int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle $ if $u\preceq v$, with equality if $u\sim v$, by corollary 15.7.5.

Inequalities between measures will usually be obtained from weak limits, that is, by testing against continuous functions. The following lemma justifies this step, and it records the inner regularity of finite measures on $X$.

Lemma 16.2.1. Let $\lambda $ and $\lambda '$ be finite positive Borel measures on $X$.

  1. For every Borel set $E\subset X$, $\lambda (E)=\sup \{ \lambda (K):K\subset E\text{ compact}\} $.

  2. Let $W\subset X$ be open, and suppose that $\int _ X\chi \, \mathrm d\lambda \leq \int _ X\chi \, \mathrm d\lambda '$ for every continuous function $\chi \geq 0$ with compact support in $W$. Then $\lambda (E)\leq \lambda '(E)$ for every Borel set $E\subset W$.

Proof. (1) Cover $X$ by finitely many coordinate charts $U_1,\ldots ,U_ N$, with homeomorphisms $\kappa _ i$ of $U_ i$ onto open sets $\Omega _ i\subset \mathbb R^{2n}$, and let $E\subset X$ be Borel and $\varepsilon >0$. The Borel sets $E_ i=(E\cap U_ i)\setminus (U_1\cup \cdots \cup U_{i-1})$ partition $E$. The image of the restriction of $\lambda $ to $U_ i$ under $\kappa _ i$ is a finite Borel measure on $\Omega _ i$, hence a Radon measure by proposition 1.1.2, so there is a compact set $L_ i\subset \kappa _ i(E_ i)$ whose complement in $\kappa _ i(E_ i)$ has measure less than $\varepsilon /N$. Then $K=\bigcup _ i\kappa _ i^{-1}(L_ i)$ is a compact subset of $E$ with $\lambda (E\setminus K)<\varepsilon $.

(2) Fix a metric $d$ on $X$ that induces its topology (lemma 7.1.4). Let $K\subset W$ be compact and nonempty; by compactness there is $r>0$ such that every point at distance at most $r$ from $K$ lies in $W$. For $k>1/r$ the continuous functions $\chi _ k=\max \{ 0,1-k\, d(\cdot ,K)\} $ take values in $[0,1]$, equal $1$ on $K$, have compact support in $W$, and decrease pointwise to $\mathbf1_ K$ as $k\to \infty $. By dominated convergence for the finite measures $\lambda $ and $\lambda '$,

\[ \lambda (K)=\lim _{k\to \infty }\int _ X\chi _ k\, \mathrm d\lambda \leq \lim _{k\to \infty }\int _ X\chi _ k\, \mathrm d\lambda '=\lambda '(K). \]

If $E\subset W$ is Borel, then by (1), $\lambda (E)=\sup _ K\lambda (K)\leq \sup _ K\lambda '(K)\leq \lambda '(E)$, the suprema being taken over the compact sets $K\subset E$. $\square$

16.2.1 Concentration on the contact set

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

We first show that the measure of an envelope is carried by its contact set with the obstacle, beginning with a comparison of the measures of two ordered potentials on the set where they agree.

Lemma 16.2.2. Let $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\leq v$. Then

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ u=v\} }\mu _ u\leq \mathbf1_{\{ u=v\} }\mu _ v. \]

Proof. For $\varepsilon >0$ put $w_\varepsilon =\max \{ u,v-\varepsilon \} $. As $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, $w_\varepsilon $ increases to $\max \{ u,v\} =v$, so $\mu _{w_\varepsilon }\to \mu _ v$ weakly along every sequence $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, by corollary 15.7.6. On the plurifine open set $\{ u>v-\varepsilon \} $ we have $w_\varepsilon =u$, hence $\mathbf1_{\{ u>v-\varepsilon \} }\mu _{w_\varepsilon }=\mathbf1_{\{ u>v-\varepsilon \} }\mu _ u$. This set contains every point of $\{ u=v\} $ at which $u$ is finite, and $\mu _ u$ does not charge the pole set of $u$. Therefore

\[ \mu _{w_\varepsilon }\geq \mathbf1_{\{ u>v-\varepsilon \} }\mu _ u \geq \mathbf1_{\{ u=v\} }\mu _ u. \]

The right side is a fixed finite measure. Integrating a continuous function $\chi \geq 0$ against both sides and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, we obtain $\int _ X\chi \, \mu _ v\geq \int _ X\chi \mathbf1_{\{ u=v\} }\, \mu _ u$, so $\mu _ v\geq \mathbf1_{\{ u=v\} }\mu _ u$ by lemma 16.2.1. Restricting both sides to $\{ u=v\} $ proves the lemma. $\square$

Lemma 16.2.3. Suppose that $\{ \theta \} $ is big. There is a proper analytic subset $A\subset X$, the pole set of a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function with analytic singularities, such that every potential with minimal singularities is locally bounded on $X\setminus A$. The set $A$ is closed and pluripolar; in particular $\mu _ u(A)=0$ for every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Proof. By proposition 14.3.5, the big class contains a Kähler current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi $ whose potential $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has analytic singularities, and we put $A=\{ \psi =-\infty \} $. Every point of $X$ has a connected coordinate neighborhood $V$ on which $\psi =c\log \sum _ i|g_ i|^2+b$, with $c>0$, $b$ bounded and holomorphic functions $g_ i$ that do not all vanish identically (definition 13.1.5). Thus $A\cap V$ is the common zero set of the $g_ i$, so $A$ is an analytic subset of $X$, different from $X$, and it is closed and pluripolar, being locally the pole set of the plurisubharmonic function $\log \sum _ i|g_ i|^2$. On $V\setminus A$ the continuous function $\sum _ i|g_ i|^2$ is positive, so $\psi $ is locally bounded on $X\setminus A$. If $\varphi $ has minimal singularities, then $\psi \leq \varphi +C'$ for a constant $C'$, so $\varphi $ is locally bounded below on $X\setminus A$; it is bounded above, being upper semicontinuous on the compact space $X$. The last assertion holds because non-pluripolar products give no mass to pluripolar sets. $\square$

Lemma 16.2.4. Suppose that $\{ \theta \} $ is big and that $f$ is bounded and lower semicontinuous. Then $u=P_\theta (f)$ has minimal singularities, and $\mu _ u$ is carried by $\{ u=f\} $.

Proof. Adding a constant to $f$, we may assume that $-C\leq f\leq 0$. Then $V_\theta -C\leq f$, so $V_\theta -C$ is a competitor, while every competitor is nonpositive and hence at most $V_\theta $. Thus $V_\theta -C\leq u\leq V_\theta $, and $u$ has minimal singularities.

Let $A$ be the proper analytic subset of lemma 16.2.3. Since $u$ has minimal singularities, it is locally bounded on $X\setminus A$, and $\mu _ u(A)=0$.

Let $x\in X\setminus A$ with $u(x)<f(x)$, and choose $\delta >0$ with $u(x)<f(x)-3\delta $. Since $u$ is upper semicontinuous and $f$ is lower semicontinuous, there is a coordinate ball $B\ni x$ with $\overline B\subset X\setminus A$ such that $u<u(x)+\delta $ and $f>f(x)-\delta $ on $\overline B$; thus $\sup _{\overline B}u\leq \inf _{\overline B}f-\delta $. Shrinking $B$, we may assume that $B\Subset B'$ for a coordinate ball $B'$ with $\overline{B'}\subset X\setminus A$ and that $\theta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$ near $\overline{B'}$ for a smooth function $h$ (lemma 7.3.1), and then, shrinking $B$ further, that the oscillation $\operatorname {osc}_{\overline B}h=\sup _{\overline B}h-\inf _{\overline B}h$ is less than $\delta $.

We show that $\mu _ u$ vanishes on $B$. Since $u$ is bounded near $\overline{B'}$, corollary 14.2.9 gives $\widetilde u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\widetilde u\geq u$, $\widetilde u=u$ on $X\setminus B$ and $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde u)^ n=0$ on $B$. The function $\widetilde u+h$ is plurisubharmonic, hence subharmonic, on $B$, and at every point $\zeta \in \partial B$ its upper limit from $B$ is at most $(\widetilde u+h)(\zeta )=(u+h)(\zeta )\leq \sup _{\overline B}(u+h)$, by upper semicontinuity. The maximum principle (proposition 3.1.4) therefore gives

\[ \widetilde u\leq \sup _{\overline B}(u+h)-h \leq \sup _{\overline B}u+\operatorname {osc}_{\overline B}h <\inf _{\overline B}f \qquad \text{on }B. \]

Hence $\widetilde u\leq f$ quasi-everywhere on $X$, so $\widetilde u$ is a competitor and $\widetilde u\leq u$. Therefore $\widetilde u=u$, and $\mu _ u=(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde u)^ n$ vanishes on $B$.

The set $\{ u<f\} $ is open, because $f-u$ is lower semicontinuous. Covering $\{ u<f\} \setminus A$ by countably many balls as above, we obtain $\mu _ u(\{ u<f\} \setminus A)=0$. Moreover $u\leq f$ quasi-everywhere by proposition 16.1.4, so the set $\{ u>f\} $ is pluripolar. Since $\mu _ u$ gives no mass to $A$ or to $\{ u>f\} $, it is carried by $\{ u=f\} $. $\square$

Theorem 16.2.5. Suppose that $\{ \theta \} $ is big, and let $f$ be a quasi-continuous Borel obstacle such that $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$ is nonempty and uniformly bounded above. Then $\mu _{P_\theta (f)}$ is carried by the contact set $\{ P_\theta (f)=f\} $, that is,

\[ \mu _{P_\theta (f)}(\{ P_\theta (f)\ne f\} )=0. \]

Proof. Write $u=P_\theta (f)$, which is a potential by proposition 16.1.4, and let $A$ be the proper analytic subset of lemma 16.2.3: every potential with minimal singularities is locally bounded on $X\setminus A$, and $A$ is closed and pluripolar. After reducing to obstacles that are bounded above, we treat bounded obstacles by approximating them with lower semicontinuous ones (Step 1), and then obstacles that are only bounded above by truncating them from below (Step 2).

Choose a constant $C_0>\sup _ Xu$ and put $\widetilde f=\min \{ f,C_0\} $, a quasi-continuous Borel function that is bounded above (lemma 16.1.2(2)). By proposition 16.1.4, $u\leq f$ quasi-everywhere, and $u<C_0$ everywhere, so $u\in \mathcal A_\theta (\widetilde f)\subset \mathcal A_\theta (f)$. Since $u$ is the largest member of $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$, it follows that $P_\theta (\widetilde f)=u$; moreover $\{ u=\widetilde f\} =\{ u=f\} $, because $u<C_0$. Replacing $f$ by $\widetilde f$, we may therefore assume that $f$ is bounded above.

Step 1: bounded obstacles. Suppose first that $|f|\leq C'$ quasi-everywhere for a constant $C'$. Then $\{ |f|>C'\} $ is a pluripolar Borel set, and replacing $f$ by $0$ on it changes neither $P_\theta (f)$ (proposition 16.1.4) nor the quasi-continuity of $f$ (lemma 16.1.2(3)); it changes the contact set only on a pluripolar set, which $\mu _ u$ does not charge. After this modification and the addition of a constant, we may therefore assume that $-C\leq f\leq 0$ everywhere.

We first construct open sets of small capacity outside which $f$ is continuous. Fix finitely many coordinate balls $D_1,\ldots ,D_ N$, compact sets $K_ i\subset D_ i$ with $\bigcup _ iK_ i=X$, and open sets $D_ i'$ with $K_ i\subset D_ i'\Subset D_ i$. For all $i$ and $k\geq 1$, the quasi-continuity of $f$ on $D_ i$ gives an open set $G_{ik}\subset D_ i'$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_{D_ i}(G_{ik})<2^{-k}$ such that $f$ is continuous on $K_ i\setminus G_{ik}$; intersecting with $D_ i'$ the open set provided by definition 9.4.1 does not change $K_ i\setminus G_{ik}$. Put

\[ G_ j=\bigcap _{k=1}^ j\bigcup _{i=1}^ NG_{ik}. \]

These open sets decrease, and $X\setminus G_ j$ is the union of the finitely many closed sets $K_ i\setminus \bigcup _{i'}G_{i'k}$, $1\leq i\leq N$, $1\leq k\leq j$. Since $f$ is continuous on each of them, it is continuous on the closed set $X\setminus G_ j$. Moreover, if $D$ is a coordinate ball and $L\subset D$ is compact, then $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_ j\cap L)\to 0$. Indeed, $L\cap \overline{D_ i'}$ is a compact subset of $D\cap D_ i$, so lemma 16.1.1(1) gives a constant $c_ i$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)\leq c_ i\operatorname {Cap}_{D_ i}(E)$ for all Borel sets $E\subset L\cap \overline{D_ i'}$. Since $G_ j\cap L\subset \bigcup _ i(G_{ij}\cap L)$ and $G_{ij}\subset D_ i'$, it follows that

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_ j\cap L)\leq \sum _{i=1}^ Nc_ i\operatorname {Cap}_{D_ i}(G_{ij})\leq 2^{-j}\sum _{i=1}^ Nc_ i. \]

Next we approximate $f$ by lower semicontinuous obstacles that agree with $f$ outside $G_ j$. Fix a metric on $X$ that induces its topology (lemma 7.1.4). By the Tietze extension theorem in the form of lemma 9.4.3, applied to the restriction of $f+C/2$ to the compact set $X\setminus G_ j$, which takes values in $[-C/2,C/2]$, the restriction of $f$ to $X\setminus G_ j$ extends to a continuous function $g_ j\colon X\to [-C,0]$. Put

\[ h_ j=\sup _{k\geq j}g_ k. \]

These functions are lower semicontinuous, take values in $[-C,0]$, and decrease with $j$. Since $G_ k\subset G_ j$ for $k\geq j$, we have $h_ j=f$ on $X\setminus G_ j$. The intersection $\bigcap _ jG_ j$ is pluripolar: if $D$ is a coordinate ball and $U\Subset D$ is open, then $U\cap \bigcap _ jG_ j$ is contained in the open sets $U\cap G_ j$, whose capacities $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ tend to zero, so it has outer capacity zero and lies in the pole set of a plurisubharmonic function on $D$ by theorem 10.1.4. Outside $\bigcap _ jG_ j$ we have $h_ j=f$ for all large $j$. Hence the limit $h$ of the $h_ j$ equals $f$ quasi-everywhere.

Finally we pass to the limit in the contact property of the envelopes $w_ j=P_\theta (h_ j)$. By proposition 16.1.4, $w_ j$ decreases to $P_\theta (h)=P_\theta (f)=u$. Since the obstacles $h_ j$ and $f$ take values in $[-C,0]$, the potential $V_\theta -C$ is a competitor for each of them, and every competitor is nonpositive, hence at most $V_\theta $; thus $V_\theta -C\leq w_ j\leq V_\theta $ and $V_\theta -C\leq u\leq V_\theta $. Fix a coordinate ball $D\Subset X\setminus A$ with a smooth potential $\rho $ of $\theta $ near $\overline D$ (such balls exist around every point of $X\setminus A$, by lemma 7.3.1), and a continuous function $\chi \geq 0$ with compact support in $D$. On $D$ the functions $w_ j$ and $u$ are bounded uniformly in $j$. By lemma 16.2.4, $\mu _{w_ j}$ is carried by $\{ w_ j=h_ j\} $, so

\[ \int _ X\chi \bigl (1-\mathrm e^{w_ j-h_ j}\bigr )\, \mu _{w_ j}=0. \]

The integrands with $h_ j$ and with $f$ are bounded uniformly in $j$ on the support of $\chi $, and they coincide outside $G_ j$. By lemma 9.4.7, applied to the bounded plurisubharmonic functions $\rho +w_ j$, the measures $\mu _{w_ j}$ of $G_ j\cap \operatorname{Supp}\chi $ are at most a fixed constant times $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_ j\cap \operatorname{Supp}\chi )$, which tends to zero, as shown above. Consequently

\[ \lim _{j\to \infty }\int _ X\chi \bigl (1-\mathrm e^{w_ j-f}\bigr )\, \mu _{w_ j}=0. \]

The weights $1-\mathrm e^{w_ j-f}$ are bounded and quasi-continuous on $D$, and they converge in capacity to $1-\mathrm e^{u-f}$. Indeed, the uniformly bounded potentials $w_ j$ decrease to $u$ and therefore converge in capacity (proposition 9.4.6), and $|\mathrm e^{w_ j-f}-\mathrm e^{u-f}|\leq \mathrm e^{C}|w_ j-u|$, because $w_ j-f$ and $u-f$ are at most $C$. The weighted form of theorem 9.4.8, applied to the bounded plurisubharmonic functions $\rho +w_ j$ on $D$, therefore gives

\[ \int _ X\chi \bigl (1-\mathrm e^{u-f}\bigr )\, \mu _ u=0. \]

The integrand is nonnegative outside the pluripolar set $\{ u>f\} $, which $\mu _ u$ does not charge, and it is positive exactly at the points of $D$ where $u<f$. Since $\chi $ is arbitrary, lemma 16.2.1(2), applied on $W=D$ to the positive measure $\max \{ 1-\mathrm e^{u-f},0\} \mu _ u$ and the zero measure, shows that this measure vanishes on $D$; hence $\mu _ u(\{ u<f\} \cap D)=0$. Covering $X\setminus A$ by such balls, and recalling that $\mu _ u$ does not charge the pluripolar sets $A$ and $\{ u>f\} $, we conclude that $\mu _ u$ is carried by $\{ u=f\} $.

Step 2: obstacles bounded above. Adding a constant, we may assume that $f\leq 0$. Put $f_ j=\max \{ f,-j\} $ and $u_ j=P_\theta (f_ j)$. The obstacles $f_ j$ are quasi-continuous by lemma 16.1.2(2), satisfy $-j\leq f_ j\leq 0$, and decrease to $f$; their families of competitors contain $V_\theta -j$ and are bounded above by $0$. By proposition 16.1.4, $u_ j$ decreases to the potential $u$, and by Step 1, $\mu _{u_ j}$ is carried by $\{ u_ j=f_ j\} $. Moreover $V_\theta -j\leq u_ j\leq V_\theta $. The potentials $u_ j$ are not uniformly bounded below, so we pass to the limit on a fixed ball with truncated potentials, and with weights that vanish where the truncation is active.

Fix a coordinate ball $D\Subset X\setminus A$ as in Step 1 and constants $M,\varepsilon >0$, and put

\[ v_ j^ M=\max \{ u_ j,V_\theta -M\} ,\qquad a_ j=\frac{v_ j^ M-V_\theta +M}{v_ j^ M-V_\theta +M+\varepsilon }, \]

and define $v^ M$ and $a$ in the same way with $u$ in place of $u_ j$. On $D$ these truncated potentials are bounded uniformly in $j$ and decrease to $v^ M$, so they converge in capacity (proposition 9.4.6). The weights satisfy $0\leq a_ j\leq 1$ and $a_ j=0$ on $\{ u_ j\leq V_\theta -M\} $. They are quasi-continuous, and they converge in capacity to $a$, since $s\mapsto (s+M)/(s+M+\varepsilon )$ is Lipschitz on $[-M,0]$.

Next put $b_ j=\mathrm e^{u_ j-f_ j}$ at the points where $u_ j$ and $f_ j$ are finite and $u_ j\leq f_ j$, and $b_ j=1$ elsewhere; define $b$ from $u$ and $f$ in the same way. Since $f\leq 0$ everywhere, $u\leq f$ and $u_ j\leq f_ j$ quasi-everywhere, and $u_ j$ and $f_ j$ are finite on $D$, the set of points of $D$ at which some $b_ j$ or $b$ is given the value $1$ is contained in the union of the pole set of $u$ and countably many pluripolar sets, so it is pluripolar.

We claim that $b_ j\to b$ in capacity on compact subsets of $D$ and that $b$ is quasi-continuous. Let $K\subset D$ be compact and $\varepsilon '>0$. Cover the exceptional pluripolar set by an open set of capacity less than $\varepsilon '$, which is possible by theorem 10.1.4, and enlarge it, using the quasi-continuity of $u$ (lemma 16.1.2(1)) and of $f$, to an open set $G$ of capacity less than $2\varepsilon '$ such that $u$ and $f$ are continuous and finite on the compact set $F=K\setminus G$. Then $u,f\geq -l$ on $F$ for some integer $l$, so $f_ j=f$ on $F$ for $j\geq l$. Since the exponential is $1$-Lipschitz on $(-\infty ,0]$, we get $|b_ j-b|\leq |u_ j-u|$ on $F$ for $j\geq l$. As $u\leq u_ j$ and $u\geq -l$ on $F$, we have $u_ j-u=\max \{ u_ j,-l\} -\max \{ u,-l\} $ there, and these truncations converge in capacity because they decrease. Hence, for every $\delta >0$, the outer capacity of $K\cap \{ |b_ j-b|>\delta \} $ is at most $2\varepsilon '$ plus a quantity that tends to zero, and $b$ is continuous on $F$. This proves the claim.

Since $a_ j$ vanishes outside the plurifine open set $\{ u_ j>V_\theta -M\} $, on which $v_ j^ M=u_ j$, and since $\mu _{u_ j}$ is carried by $\{ u_ j=f_ j\} $, where $b_ j=1$ up to a pluripolar set, we have

\[ a_ j(1-b_ j)\mu _{v_ j^ M}=a_ j(1-b_ j)\mu _{u_ j}=0. \]

The weights $a_ j(1-b_ j)$ are bounded and converge in capacity to the quasi-continuous function $a(1-b)$. The weighted form of theorem 9.4.8, applied to the bounded plurisubharmonic functions $\rho +v_ j^ M$, gives $a(1-b)\mu _{v^ M}=0$ on $D$, and plurifine locality turns this into $a(1-b)\mu _ u=0$ on $D$. Let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ and then $M\to \infty $. Since $V_\theta $ is finite on $D$, the weights $a$ tend to $1$ at every point of $D$ where $u$ is finite. They are bounded by $1$, and $\mu _ u$ does not charge the pole set of $u$, so dominated convergence gives $(1-b)\mu _ u=0$ on $D$. Thus $u=f$ holds $\mu _ u$-almost everywhere on $D$. Covering $X\setminus A$ by such balls and using $\mu _ u(A)=0$ completes the proof. $\square$

In corollary 17.4.21 of the next chapter, the assumption that $\{ \theta \} $ is big is removed.

Corollary 16.2.6. Suppose that $\{ \theta \} $ is big, let $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and suppose that $w=P_\theta (u,v)\ne -\infty $. Then

\[ \mu _ w\leq \mathbf1_{\{ w=u\} }\mu _ u +\mathbf1_{\{ w=v\} }\mu _ v. \]

More generally, for finitely many potentials $u_1,\ldots ,u_ r$ such that $w=P_\theta (\min _ ju_ j)\ne -\infty $, the measure $\mu _ w$ is carried by $\bigcup _ j\{ w=u_ j\} $.

Proof. By lemma 16.1.2, potentials are quasi-continuous, and hence so is the obstacle $\min \{ u,v\} $. Its family of competitors is nonempty, because $w\ne -\infty $, and bounded above by $\sup _ Xu$. By theorem 16.2.5, $\mu _ w$ is carried by $\{ w=\min \{ u,v\} \} \subset \{ w=u\} \cup \{ w=v\} $. Since $w\leq u$ and $w\leq v$ everywhere, lemma 16.2.2 gives $\mathbf1_{\{ w=u\} }\mu _ w\leq \mathbf1_{\{ w=u\} }\mu _ u$ and $\mathbf1_{\{ w=v\} }\mu _ w\leq \mathbf1_{\{ w=v\} }\mu _ v$. Adding these inequalities proves the first assertion; the intersection of the two contact sets is counted twice, which is harmless for an upper bound. The same argument, applied to the obstacle $\min _ ju_ j$, proves the last assertion. $\square$

16.2.2 Obstacles with bounded metric Laplacian

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

The results above show that the Monge–Ampère measure of an envelope is carried by the contact set, but they do not identify the measure there. For obstacles with bounded Laplacian we now compute the measure on the contact set from the second derivatives of the obstacle. Recall from section 14.1 that $L=\mathrm d^*\mathrm d$ is the Laplace–Beltrami operator of the Riemannian metric defined by the fixed Kähler form $\omega $; with our sign convention it is nonnegative, and for the Euclidean metric it equals $-\Delta $. We call $L$ the metric Laplacian. A function $f$ on an open set $U\subset X$ has (locally) bounded metric Laplacian if the distribution $Lf$ on $U$ is a (locally) bounded measurable function.

By the trace identity $\Lambda \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f=-Lf/(4\pi )$ of lemma 14.1.6, where $\Lambda $ is the trace with respect to $\omega $, a bound for $Lf$ is a bound for the trace of the complex Hessian of $f$. In local holomorphic coordinates, write $\omega =\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\sum _{i,j}h_{i\bar j}\, \mathrm dz_ i\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j$ and let $(h^{i\bar j})$ be the inverse matrix, normalized by $\sum _ jh^{i\bar j}h_{l\bar j}=\delta _{il}$; then (14.14), applied with $\omega '=\omega $, gives

16.1
\begin{equation} Lf=-4\pi \sum _{i,j}h^{i\bar j}f_{i\bar j} \end{equation}

for every distribution $f$ in the coordinate domain. For a $\theta $-psh function $u$ the current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is positive, and positivity turns this trace bound into bounds for all complex Hessian coefficients. A general obstacle has no such positivity, and if $n\geq 2$ its complex Hessian need not be bounded (example 16.2.7). Nevertheless it has square-integrable weak second derivatives (lemma 16.2.8), and on the set where it agrees with a potential that has bounded metric Laplacian, these coincide almost everywhere with the second derivatives of the potential. This gives the contact formula of proposition 16.2.9. The $L^ p$ estimate of proposition 16.2.11 shows moreover that such an obstacle has weak second derivatives in every $L^ p$ with $p<\infty $, so that wedge products of the corresponding weak forms are integrable on all of $X$ (corollary 16.2.12).

Example 16.2.7. Let $n\geq 2$ and $x_0\in X$. Choose holomorphic coordinates $z$ centered at $x_0$ in which $h_{i\bar j}(0)=\delta _{ij}$, which is possible after a complex-linear change of coordinates, and choose $0<r_0<\mathrm e^{-1}$ such that the coordinates are defined on a neighborhood of $\{ |z|\leq r_0\} $. On $0<|z|<1$ put

\[ \ell (z)=\log (-\log |z|),\qquad P(z)=|z_1|^2-|z_2|^2,\qquad \phi =P\ell , \]

and $\phi (0)=0$. Write $r=|z|$. Since $\partial _{z_ i}\log r=\bar z_ i/(2r^2)$ and $\partial _{\bar z_ j}(r^2\log r)=z_ j(\log r+\frac12)$, we have

\[ \partial _{z_ i}\ell =\frac{\bar z_ i}{2r^2\log r},\qquad \partial _{z_ i}\partial _{z_ j}\ell =-\frac{(\log r+\frac12)\bar z_ i\bar z_ j}{2r^4(\log r)^2}, \]
\[ \ell _{i\bar j}=\frac{\delta _{ij}}{2r^2\log r}-\frac{(\log r+\frac12)\bar z_ iz_ j}{2r^4(\log r)^2}. \]

For $0<r<r_0$ we have $\log r<-1$, hence $|\log r+\frac12|\leq |\log r|$, and these formulas give $|\nabla \ell |\leq C/(r|\log r|)$ and $|D^2\ell |\leq C/(r^2|\log r|)$ for the real gradient and Hessian, with a constant $C$ depending only on $n$. Moreover $|P|\leq r^2$, $|\nabla P|\leq 2r$, the real Hessian of $P$ is constant, $P_{1\bar1}=1$, $P_{2\bar2}=-1$, and all other $P_{i\bar j}$ vanish. By the Leibniz rule, on $0<r<r_0$,

\[ |\nabla \phi |\leq 2r\ell +\frac{Cr}{|\log r|},\qquad |D^2\phi -\ell D^2P|\leq \frac{C}{|\log r|},\qquad \Bigl |\phi _{i\bar j}-\ell P_{i\bar j}\Bigr |\leq \frac{C}{|\log r|}. \]

In particular $\phi $ is continuously differentiable on $\{ |z|<r_0\} $, with $\nabla \phi (0)=0$, since $|\phi |\leq r^2\ell $ and $\nabla \phi \to 0$ as $z\to 0$; and its pointwise second derivatives off the origin are bounded by $C(1+\ell )\leq C(1-\log |z|)$, hence locally integrable.

These pointwise derivatives are the distributional second derivatives of $\phi $. Indeed, let $\eta $ be a smooth function on $\mathbb R$ with $\eta =0$ on $(-\infty ,1]$ and $\eta =1$ on $[2,\infty )$, put $\eta _\varepsilon (z)=\eta (|z|/\varepsilon )$, and let $\psi $ be a test function on $\{ |z|<r_0\} $. Since $\phi $ is $C^1$, we have $\int \phi \, \partial _ a\partial _ b\psi =-\int \partial _ a\phi \, \partial _ b\psi $ for real coordinate derivatives $\partial _ a,\partial _ b$. Splitting $\psi =\eta _\varepsilon \psi +(1-\eta _\varepsilon )\psi $ and integrating by parts once more in the first term, where $\phi $ is smooth, we get

\[ \int \phi \, \partial _ a\partial _ b\psi =\int \partial _ b\partial _ a\phi \, \eta _\varepsilon \psi -\int \partial _ a\phi \, \partial _ b\bigl ((1-\eta _\varepsilon )\psi \bigr ). \]

The last integral is bounded by $\sup _{|z|\leq 2\varepsilon }|\nabla \phi |\cdot C_\psi (\varepsilon ^{-1}+1)\varepsilon ^{2n}$, which tends to zero, and the first tends to $\int \partial _ b\partial _ a\phi \, \psi $ by dominated convergence.

Choose a smooth function $\chi $ on $X$ with support in $\{ |z|<r_0\} $ that equals $1$ on $\{ |z|\leq r_0/2\} $, and put $f=\chi \phi $, extended by zero. Then $f$ is continuous. On $\{ |z|<r_0/2\} $, (16.1) and the estimates above give

\[ \Bigl |\frac{Lf}{4\pi }\Bigr |\leq \ell \, |h^{1\bar1}-h^{2\bar2}|+\frac{C'}{|\log r|}\leq C''\bigl (r\ell +1\bigr ), \]

because $h^{1\bar1}-h^{2\bar2}$ is smooth and vanishes at the origin; the right side is bounded, since $r\ell \to 0$ as $r\to 0$, and elsewhere $f$ is smooth. Hence $f$ has bounded metric Laplacian. On the other hand $f_{1\bar1}=\phi _{1\bar1}\geq \ell -C/|\log r|$ near $x_0$, which is unbounded. Thus the complex Hessian of $f$ is not locally bounded.

We use the Sobolev spaces $W^{k,p}$ of section 6.2. For an open set $U\subset X$, a function on $U$ belongs to $W^{k,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$ if it belongs to $W^{k,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ in every holomorphic chart of $U$. Since membership in $W^{k,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ is a local property, lemma 6.2.4(2), applied to the coordinate changes, shows that it suffices to check this in the charts of one atlas of $U$. To see that the property is local, let $D\subset \mathbb R^ d$ be open, covered by open sets $D_1,D_2,\ldots $ (an open cover of $D$ has a countable subcover), and let $v\in W^{k,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D_ i)$ for every $i$. For $|\alpha |\leq k$, the $L^ p_{\mathrm{loc}}$ functions representing $\partial ^\alpha v$ on $D_ i$ and on $D_ j$ agree almost everywhere on $D_ i\cap D_ j$ (proposition 1.4.3(4)), so they define a measurable function $g_\alpha $ on $D$. It belongs to $L^ p_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, because every compact subset of $D$ is covered by finitely many compact balls, each contained in some $D_ i$. Finally, the distribution $\partial ^\alpha v-g_\alpha $ vanishes on every $D_ i$, hence on $D$ (lemma 1.4.2).

Let $(U_ i,\varphi _ i)$ and $(\chi _ i)$ be the charts and the partition of unity that define the norm of $W^{k,p}(X)$. For an open set $U'\subset X$ we put $\| f\| _{W^{k,p}(U')}=\sum _ i\| (\chi _ if)\circ \varphi _ i^{-1}\| _{W^{k,p}(\varphi _ i(U'\cap U_ i))}$. Every $v\in W^{k,p}(X)$ belongs to $W^{k,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)$, with a bound: for every holomorphic chart $\psi \colon U_0\to \mathbb C^ n$ and every open set $U'\Subset U_0$,

16.2
\begin{equation} \| v\circ \psi ^{-1}\| _{W^{k,p}(\psi (U'))}\leq C\| v\| _{W^{k,p}(X)}, \end{equation}

with $C$ independent of $v$. Indeed, let $\eta $ be a smooth function on $X$ with compact support in $U_0$ that equals $1$ on $U'$ (proposition 7.1.3). The charts $\psi ,\varphi _1,\varphi _2,\ldots $ with the partition of unity $\eta ,(1-\eta )\chi _1,(1-\eta )\chi _2,\ldots $ are another admissible choice in section 6.2, so the norm they define is at most a constant times $\| v\| _{W^{k,p}(X)}$; one of its terms is $\| (\eta v)\circ \psi ^{-1}\| _{W^{k,p}(\psi (U_0))}$, which dominates the left side of (16.2).

Finally, the Hilbert spaces $H^ r$, $r\geq 0$, of section 14.1 are contained in $W^{r,2}(X)$, with $\| u\| _{W^{r,2}(X)}\leq C\| u\| _{H^ r}$. Indeed, the charts and the cutoff functions that define $H^ r$ are an admissible choice in section 6.2, so, up to a constant factor, the norm of $W^{r,2}(X)$ is the sum of the $W^{r,2}$ norms of the products of $u$ with these cutoff functions, written in the corresponding charts. By lemma 14.1.1(2), for $u\in H^ r$ these products have square-integrable distributional derivatives of order at most $r$, and this sum is at most $C\| u\| _{H^ r}$.

The following lemma is the variable-coefficient analogue of lemma 9.12.2.

Lemma 16.2.8. Let $U\subset X$ be open, and let $f\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$ be such that its distributional metric Laplacian $Lf$ belongs to $L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$. Then $f\in W^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$. More precisely, for open sets $U'\Subset V\Subset U$ there is a constant $C$, depending only on $U'$, $V$ and $\omega $ (and on the fixed charts defining the norms), such that

\[ \| f\| _{W^{2,2}(U')}\leq C\bigl (\| f\| _{L^2(V)}+\| Lf\| _{L^2(V)}\bigr ). \]

In particular, every continuous function on $U$ with locally bounded metric Laplacian belongs to $W^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$.

Proof. Fix $c>0$. By lemma 14.1.3(3), applied to functions, on which $\mathcal L=L$, the operator $P=L+c$ is a bijection of the space of distributions on $X$ onto itself, and its inverse maps $H^{-1}$ boundedly into $H^1$ and $H^0=L^2$ (lemma 14.1.1(2)) boundedly into $H^2$. Since no derivative of $f$ is known in advance, we gain regularity in two steps, using two cutoff functions.

For a smooth function $\chi $ on $X$, the Leibniz rules $\mathrm d(\chi u)=\chi \, \mathrm du+u\, \mathrm d\chi $ and $\mathrm d^*(\chi \alpha )=\chi \, \mathrm d^*\alpha -\langle \alpha ,\mathrm d\chi \rangle $, for smooth functions $u$ and real $1$-forms $\alpha $, give

16.3
\begin{equation} L(\chi u)=\chi Lu+[L,\chi ]u,\qquad [L,\chi ]u=-2\langle \mathrm d\chi ,\mathrm du\rangle +(L\chi )u, \end{equation}

where $\langle \cdot ,\cdot \rangle $ is the pointwise inner product of real $1$-forms, extended bilinearly. Since $L$ and multiplication by $\chi $ are formally self-adjoint, the transpose of $[L,\chi ]$ is $-[L,\chi ]$, and (16.3) extends to distributions $u$, with $\langle [L,\chi ]u,\phi \rangle =-\langle u,[L,\chi ]\phi \rangle $ for smooth $\phi $.

By proposition 7.1.3, choose smooth functions $\chi _0,\chi _1$ on $X$ with compact supports in $V$ such that $\chi _1=1$ on a neighborhood of $\overline{U'}$ and $\chi _0=1$ on a neighborhood of $\operatorname{Supp}\chi _1$. Extend $\chi _0f$ by zero to a function on $X$. By (16.3),

\[ P(\chi _0f)=\chi _0(Lf+cf)+[L,\chi _0]f. \]

The first term lies in $L^2$, with norm at most $\| Lf\| _{L^2(V)}+c\| f\| _{L^2(V)}$, hence in $H^{-1}$ with a comparable norm. For smooth $\phi $, the function $[L,\chi _0]\phi $ is a first-order expression in $\phi $ with smooth coefficients supported in $V$, so

\[ |\langle [L,\chi _0]f,\phi \rangle |=|\langle f,[L,\chi _0]\phi \rangle |\leq C\| f\| _{L^2(V)}\| \phi \| _{H^1}, \]

because the norm of $H^1$ dominates the $L^2$ norms of the first derivatives on compact subsets of the charts (lemma 14.1.1(2)). Hence $[L,\chi _0]f\in H^{-1}$, and the right side of the last identity has $H^{-1}$ norm at most $C(\| Lf\| _{L^2(V)}+\| f\| _{L^2(V)})$. Applying $P^{-1}$, we obtain $\chi _0f\in H^1$ with the same bound.

In particular $f$ has square-integrable first derivatives on the interior of $\{ \chi _0=1\} $, which contains $\operatorname{Supp}\chi _1$, with $L^2$ norm bounded in the same way. Therefore the distribution $[L,\chi _1]f=-2\langle \mathrm d\chi _1,\mathrm df\rangle +(L\chi _1)f$ is a square-integrable function, and in the identity

\[ P(\chi _1f)=\chi _1(Lf+cf)+[L,\chi _1]f \]

the right side lies in $L^2$, with norm at most $C(\| Lf\| _{L^2(V)}+\| f\| _{L^2(V)})$. Since $P^{-1}$ is bounded from $L^2$ to $H^2$, it follows that $\chi _1f\in H^2\subset W^{2,2}(X)$, with this bound for its norm in $W^{2,2}(X)$. Since $\chi _1=1$ on $U'$, this proves the estimate.

Finally, let $\psi \colon U_0\to \mathbb C^ n$ be a holomorphic chart with $U_0\subset U$, and let $U''\Subset U_0$ be open. Applying the preceding argument with $U'=U''$ and some open $V$ with $U''\Subset V\Subset U$, we find $\chi _1f\in W^{2,2}(X)$ with $\chi _1=1$ on $U''$, so $f\circ \psi ^{-1}\in W^{2,2}(\psi (U''))$ by (16.2). Hence $f\in W^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$. The last assertion holds because continuous functions and locally bounded measurable functions are locally square-integrable. $\square$

For the contact formula we work in a coordinate ball. Recall that $D(A_1,\ldots ,A_ n)$ denotes the mixed discriminant of Hermitian $n\times n$ matrices, defined in section 7.2 (see lemma 7.2.2 for its basic properties), that $\mathrm dV_{2n}$ is Lebesgue measure in the coordinates, and that $(u_{a\bar b})$, with $u_{a\bar b}=\partial _{z_ a}\partial _{\bar z_ b}u$, is the complex Hessian matrix of $u$ (section 7.1).

Proposition 16.2.9. Let $U\subset X$ be open. For $1\leq j\leq n$, let $\theta _ j$ be a closed smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $U$, let $u_ j$ be a continuous $\theta _ j$-psh function on $U$ with locally bounded metric Laplacian, and let $f_ j$ be a real Borel function on $U$ that belongs to $W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$. By lemma 16.2.8, this holds, for instance, if $f_ j$ is continuous and has locally bounded metric Laplacian. Put

\[ E=\bigcap _{j=1}^ n\{ u_ j=f_ j\} . \]

Let $W\Subset U$ be a coordinate ball, choose smooth real functions $\rho _ j$ on $W$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho _ j=\theta _ j$, and let

\[ A_ j=\bigl ((\rho _ j+u_ j)_{a\bar b}\bigr )_{a,b}, \qquad B_ j=\bigl ((\rho _ j+f_ j)_{a\bar b}\bigr )_{a,b} \]

be the weak complex Hessian matrices, defined almost everywhere on $W$. Then each $A_ j$ is positive semidefinite almost everywhere and locally bounded, and on $W$

\[ \begin{aligned} \bigwedge _{j=1}^ n(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j) & =\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}D(A_1,\ldots ,A_ n)\, \mathrm dV_{2n},\\ \mathbf1_ E\bigwedge _{j=1}^ n(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j) & =\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}\mathbf1_ E D(B_1,\ldots ,B_ n)\, \mathrm dV_{2n}. \end{aligned} \]

In particular the density $\mathbf1_ ED(B_1,\ldots ,B_ n)$ is nonnegative and locally bounded, although the $f_ j$ need not be $\theta _ j$-psh.

Proof. The potentials $\rho _ j$ exist by lemma 13.1.2. By lemma 16.2.8, all $u_ j$ belong to $W^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)\subset W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$, so the matrices $A_ j$ and $B_ j$ are defined almost everywhere, and the positive current $T_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\rho _ j+u_ j)$ has coefficients in $L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$.

We first show that $A_ j$ is positive semidefinite almost everywhere. For each $\xi \in \mathbb C^ n$ the distribution $\sum _{a,b}(\rho _ j+u_ j)_{a\bar b}\xi _ a\bar\xi _ b$ is positive, because $T_ j$ is a positive current; since it is a locally integrable function, it is nonnegative almost everywhere, by proposition 1.4.3(3),(4) and lemma 1.4.1(2) (its mollifications are nonnegative and converge to it in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$). We apply this to the countably many $\xi $ with rational real and imaginary parts. Outside the union of the corresponding null sets, $\sum _{a,b}(A_ j)_{ab}\xi _ a\bar\xi _ b\geq 0$ for all such $\xi $, hence for all $\xi \in \mathbb C^ n$, because these $\xi $ are dense and a quadratic form on $\mathbb C^ n$ is continuous.

Next we bound the coefficients. By the trace identity (14.13),

\[ \Lambda T_ j=\Lambda \theta _ j-\frac{Lu_ j}{4\pi }, \]

which is bounded on $W$, since $Lu_ j$ is locally bounded and $\overline W$ is compact. Since $T_ j=\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\sum _{a,b}(A_ j)_{ab}\, \mathrm dz_ a\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ b$, the function $\Lambda T_ j$ is the trace $\sum _{a,b}h^{a\bar b}(A_ j)_{ab}=\operatorname {tr}(H^{-1}A_ j)$ of $A_ j$ relative to $h$, as in (14.14), where $H=(h_{a\bar b})$. As $\overline W$ is compact, $H\leq CI$ on $W$ for a constant $C$, so $H^{-1}\geq C^{-1}I$; since $A_ j$ is positive semidefinite, $\operatorname {tr}(H^{-1}A_ j)=\operatorname {tr}(A_ j^{1/2}H^{-1}A_ j^{1/2})\geq C^{-1}\operatorname {tr}A_ j$ almost everywhere on $W$. For a positive semidefinite Hermitian matrix every diagonal entry is at most the trace, and every entry satisfies $|a_{ab}|^2\leq a_{aa}a_{bb}$, by the positivity of the principal $2\times 2$ minors. Therefore $A_ j$ is bounded on $W$.

The functions $\rho _ j+u_ j$ are continuous and plurisubharmonic on $W$, and they have bounded complex Hessian in the sense of section 9.12. The first identity is therefore the first part of proposition 9.12.4. The functions $\rho _ j+f_ j$ are real Borel functions in $W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(W)$, and adding $\rho _ j$ to both $u_ j$ and $f_ j$ does not change the contact set $\{ u_ j=f_ j\} $. Hence the second part of proposition 9.12.4 gives the second identity. Moreover proposition 9.12.3, applied to $\rho _ j+u_ j$ and $\rho _ j+f_ j$, shows that $A_ j=B_ j$ almost everywhere on $\{ u_ j=f_ j\} $, hence on $E$. Consequently $\mathbf1_ ED(B_1,\ldots ,B_ n)=\mathbf1_ ED(A_1,\ldots ,A_ n)$ almost everywhere. This density is nonnegative, since the mixed discriminant of positive semidefinite matrices is nonnegative (lemma 7.2.2(3)), and it is bounded, since $D$ is a polynomial in the entries of the bounded matrices $A_ j$. $\square$

The contact formula involves the weak complex Hessians of an obstacle only on the contact set. Off this set the second derivatives of an obstacle with bounded metric Laplacian may be unbounded, and to give a meaning to the wedge products of the forms $\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j$ on all of $X$ we need more integrability. We now prove that all weak second derivatives of such an obstacle lie in $L^ p$ for every $p<\infty $. This belongs to the $L^ p$ theory of the elliptic operator $L$ on the compact manifold $X$, and we derive it from the Euclidean estimate (6.7) by freezing coefficients. We write $C^{1,\alpha }(X)$ for the space of functions on $X$ that belong to $C^{1,\alpha }_{\mathrm{loc}}$ in every chart (section 6.2). As there, matrices carry the Hilbert–Schmidt norm, and for a matrix $A=(A^{\mu \sigma })$ of functions and a function $w$ we write $A:D^2w=\sum _{\mu ,\sigma }A^{\mu \sigma }\partial _\mu \partial _\sigma w$.

Lemma 16.2.10. Let $d\geq 1$, $1<p<\infty $ and $\lambda >0$. There are constants $\delta _0>0$ and $C$, depending only on $d$, $p$ and $\lambda $, with the following property. Let $B\subset \mathbb R^ d$ be a ball with center $x_ B$, and let $A$ be a measurable function on $B$ with values in the real symmetric $d\times d$ matrices such that

\[ A(x_ B)\geq \lambda I,\qquad |A(x)-A(x_ B)|\leq \delta _0\quad \text{for }x\in B. \]

Then every $w\in W^{2,p}(\mathbb R^ d)$ that vanishes almost everywhere outside a compact subset of $B$ satisfies

\[ \| D^2w\| _ p\leq C\| A:D^2w\| _{L^ p(B)}. \]

Proof. First let $A_0$ be a constant real symmetric matrix with $A_0\geq \lambda I$, and put $M=A_0^{-1/2}$, a symmetric matrix of operator norm at most $\lambda ^{-1/2}$. For $v\in C_ c^\infty (\mathbb R^ d)$ let $\tilde v(y)=v(M^{-1}y)$, so that $v(x)=\tilde v(Mx)$. Then $D^2v(x)=M\, D^2\tilde v(Mx)\, M$ and $A_0:D^2v(x)=\operatorname {tr}(MA_0M\, D^2\tilde v(Mx))=\Delta \tilde v(Mx)$. Since $|MNM|\leq \lambda ^{-1}|N|$ for every matrix $N$, the change of variables $y=Mx$ and (6.7) give

\[ \begin{aligned} \| D^2v\| _ p& \leq \lambda ^{-1}|\det M|^{-1/p}\| D^2\tilde v\| _ p\\ & \leq \lambda ^{-1}C_{d,p}|\det M|^{-1/p}\| \Delta \tilde v\| _ p =\lambda ^{-1}C_{d,p}\| A_0:D^2v\| _ p. \end{aligned} \]

This estimate extends to every $w\in W^{2,p}(\mathbb R^ d)$ that vanishes almost everywhere outside a compact set $K$. Indeed, for $0<\varepsilon \leq 1$ the mollifications $w*\rho _\varepsilon $ are test functions supported in the compact set $K'=\{ x:\operatorname {dist}(x,K)\leq 1\} $, their second derivatives are $(\partial _\mu \partial _\sigma w)*\rho _\varepsilon $, by proposition 1.4.3(1), and these converge to $\partial _\mu \partial _\sigma w$ in $L^ p(K')$, by lemma 1.4.1(2), hence in $L^ p(\mathbb R^ d)$, since all these functions vanish almost everywhere outside $K'$. Applying the estimate to $v=w*\rho _\varepsilon $ and letting $\varepsilon \to 0$, we obtain it for $w$.

Now let $A$ and $w$ be as in the statement, put $C_1=\lambda ^{-1}C_{d,p}$, $\delta _0=1/(2C_1)$, and $A_0=A(x_ B)$. Since $|\sum _{\mu ,\sigma }m_{\mu \sigma }m'_{\mu \sigma }|\leq |m|\, |m'|$ for matrices $m$ and $m'$, we have $|A_0:D^2w-A:D^2w|\leq \delta _0|D^2w|$ almost everywhere on $B$, and both sides vanish outside $B$. Hence

\[ \| D^2w\| _ p\leq C_1\| A_0:D^2w\| _ p\leq C_1\| A:D^2w\| _{L^ p(B)}+\tfrac 12\| D^2w\| _ p, \]

and since $\| D^2w\| _ p<\infty $ we obtain the lemma with $C=2C_1$. $\square$

We apply the lemma to complex Hessian operators. Let $U\subset \mathbb C^ n=\mathbb R^{2n}$ be open, with real coordinates $x_{2i-1}=\operatorname{Re}z_ i$, $x_{2i}=\operatorname{Im}z_ i$, and let $(a^{i\bar j})$ be a function on $U$ with values in $n\times n$ matrices such that $\overline{a^{i\bar j}}=a^{j\bar i}$. Since $\partial _{z_ i}=\frac12(\partial _{2i-1}-\mathrm i\partial _{2i})$ and $\partial _{\bar z_ j}=\frac12(\partial _{2j-1}+\mathrm i\partial _{2j})$, the operator $\sum _{i,j}a^{i\bar j}\partial _{z_ i}\partial _{\bar z_ j}$ equals $\sum _{\mu ,\sigma }c^{\mu \sigma }\partial _\mu \partial _\sigma $ with complex coefficients $c^{\mu \sigma }$. For $\xi \in \mathbb R^{2n}$ they satisfy $\sum _{\mu ,\sigma }c^{\mu \sigma }\xi _\mu \xi _\sigma =\frac14\sum _{i,j}a^{i\bar j}\zeta _ i\bar\zeta _ j$, where $\zeta _ i=\xi _{2i-1}-\mathrm i\xi _{2i}$. The operator maps real functions to real functions, because $\overline{a^{i\bar j}\partial _{z_ i}\partial _{\bar z_ j}w}=a^{j\bar i}\partial _{\bar z_ i}\partial _{z_ j}w$ for real $w$. Hence, on real functions,

16.8
\begin{equation} \sum _{i,j}a^{i\bar j}\partial _{z_ i}\partial _{\bar z_ j}w=A:D^2w,\qquad A\xi \cdot \xi =\frac14\sum _{i,j}a^{i\bar j}\zeta _ i\bar\zeta _ j, \end{equation}

where $A$ is the symmetric part of the real part of $(c^{\mu \sigma })$. Thus $A$ is real symmetric, $|A(x)-A(y)|\leq |a(x)-a(y)|$, and since $|\zeta |=|\xi |$, a lower bound $\sum a^{i\bar j}\zeta _ i\bar\zeta _ j\geq \lambda |\zeta |^2$ gives $A\geq (\lambda /4)I$. By (16.1), in a holomorphic chart the metric Laplacian acts on real functions as $L=-4\pi \, A:D^2$, where $A$ is obtained in this way from $a^{i\bar j}=h^{i\bar j}$; in particular $L$ has no terms of order less than two.

Proposition 16.2.11. Let $1<p<\infty $, and let $f\in L^ p(X)$ be such that its distributional metric Laplacian $Lf$ belongs to $L^ p(X)$. Then $f\in W^{2,p}(X)$, and

16.9
\begin{equation} \| f\| _{W^{2,p}(X)}\leq C_{X,\omega ,p} \bigl (\| Lf\| _{L^ p(X)}+\| f\| _{L^ p(X)}\bigr ), \end{equation}

where the constant $C_{X,\omega ,p}$ depends only on $X$, $\omega $ and $p$ (and on the fixed charts defining the norm). In particular, a continuous function on $X$ with bounded metric Laplacian belongs to $W^{2,p}(X)$ for every finite $p\geq 1$ and to $C^{1,\alpha }(X)$ for every $0<\alpha <1$.

Proof. We first treat smooth functions and then pass to the general case through the inverse of $L+c$.

Step 1: the estimate for smooth real functions. Let $d=2n$. Fix finitely many holomorphic charts and compact subsets $K_\iota $ of their domains whose interiors cover $X$. By (16.8), in these charts $L=-4\pi \, A:D^2$ on real functions, with smooth real symmetric matrices $A$; by compactness there is $\lambda >0$ with $A\geq \lambda I$ on every $K_\iota $. Let $\delta _0$ and $C$ be the constants of lemma 16.2.10 for $d$, $p$ and $\lambda $. Since $A$ is uniformly continuous on a compact neighborhood of each $K_\iota $, there is $r>0$ such that, for every $x\in K_\iota $, the ball $B(x,r)$ lies in that neighborhood and $|A(y)-A(x)|\leq \delta _0$ for $y\in B(x,r)$. Cover $X$ by finitely many such balls $B_\nu $, and take a smooth partition of unity $(\chi _\nu )$ with $\operatorname{Supp}\chi _\nu $ a compact subset of $B_\nu $ (proposition 7.1.3).

Let $f$ be smooth and real, and let $v_\nu =\chi _\nu f$, written in the chart of $B_\nu $ and extended by zero. By lemma 16.2.10, $\| D^2v_\nu \| _ p\leq C\| A:D^2v_\nu \| _ p=(C/4\pi )\| Lv_\nu \| _ p$. By (16.3), $Lv_\nu =\chi _\nu Lf+[L,\chi _\nu ]f$, where the commutator involves only $f$ and its first derivatives. Summation therefore gives

\[ S_2:=\sum _\nu \| D^2v_\nu \| _ p \leq C\bigl (\| Lf\| _ p+\| \nabla f\| _ p+\| f\| _ p\bigr ). \]

Here and below the $L^ p$ norms of $f$ and $\nabla f$ on $X$ may be computed in the charts or with the metric of $\omega $; on the fixed finite cover these choices give equivalent norms. Since $f=\sum _\nu v_\nu $, the norm $\| \nabla f\| _ p$ is at most a fixed multiple of $\sum _\nu \| \nabla v_\nu \| _ p$. By lemma 6.2.5, for every $\varepsilon >0$,

\[ \sum _\nu \| \nabla v_\nu \| _ p\leq \varepsilon S_2+C_\varepsilon \| f\| _ p. \]

Combining the last three estimates and choosing $\varepsilon $ small, we can absorb $S_2$, which is finite because $f$ is smooth. This gives $S_2\leq C(\| Lf\| _ p+\| f\| _ p)$ and then $\| \nabla f\| _ p\leq C(\| Lf\| _ p+\| f\| _ p)$. Since the norm of $W^{2,p}(X)$ may be computed, up to equivalence, with the charts of the balls $B_\nu $ and the partition of unity $(\chi _\nu )$ (section 6.2), this proves (16.9) for smooth real $f$.

Step 2: an $L^ p$ bound for the inverse. Fix $c>0$ and put $P=L+c$. For a smooth real function $\psi $, lemma 14.1.3(3) gives a unique distributional solution $u=P^{-1}\psi $, which lies in every $H^ r$ and is therefore smooth, by lemma 14.1.1(3); it is real since $P$ is a real operator. For $\eta >0$ put

\[ \phi _\eta (s)=s(s^2+\eta )^{(p-2)/2}, \qquad \phi _\eta '(s)= \bigl ((p-1)s^2+\eta \bigr )(s^2+\eta )^{(p-4)/2}\geq 0, \qquad s\in \mathbb R. \]

The function $\phi _\eta $ is smooth, so we may multiply the equation $Lu+cu=\psi $ by $\phi _\eta (u)$ and integrate. Since $\mathrm d^*$ is the formal adjoint of $\mathrm d$, we have $\int _ X(Lu)\phi _\eta (u)\, \mathrm dV=\int _ X\phi _\eta '(u)|\mathrm du|^2\, \mathrm dV\geq 0$, and therefore

\[ c\int _ Xu\phi _\eta (u)\, \mathrm dV\leq \int _ X\psi \phi _\eta (u)\, \mathrm dV. \]

As $\eta \downarrow 0$, $u\phi _\eta (u)\to |u|^ p$ and $\phi _\eta (u)\to u|u|^{p-2}$ pointwise, the latter being $0$ where $u=0$. For $p\geq 2$ and $\eta \leq 1$ both functions are bounded by $(1+u^2)^{p/2}$, which is bounded since $u$ is continuous on the compact manifold $X$; for $1<p<2$ we have $(u^2+\eta )^{(p-2)/2}\leq |u|^{p-2}$ where $u\neq 0$, so they are bounded by $|u|^ p$ and $|u|^{p-1}$. Dominated convergence and Hölder's inequality therefore give $c\| u\| _ p^ p\leq \| \psi \| _ p\| u\| _ p^{p-1}$, that is (if $u\neq 0$; otherwise there is nothing to prove),

\[ \| P^{-1}\psi \| _ p\leq c^{-1}\| \psi \| _ p. \]

Applying Step 1 to $u$, for which $Lu=\psi -cu$, we conclude that $\| P^{-1}\psi \| _{W^{2,p}}\leq C\| \psi \| _ p$.

Step 3: the general case. Let $f$ be real, put $\psi =Lf+cf\in L^ p(X)$, and choose smooth real functions $\psi _ j\to \psi $ in $L^ p(X)$, using chart cutoffs and mollification (lemma 1.4.1). By Step 2 the solutions $u_ j=P^{-1}\psi _ j$ form a Cauchy sequence in $W^{2,p}(X)$. This space is complete: in each chart the derivatives of order at most two converge in $L^ p$, and testing against smooth functions identifies the limits with the weak derivatives of the limit function. Let $u$ be the limit. Then $Pu_ j\to Pu$ in the sense of distributions, so $Pu=\psi $. Thus $P(u-f)=0$, and since $P$ is injective on distributions (lemma 14.1.3(3)), $u=f$. Passing to the limit in the estimate of Step 2 gives $\| f\| _{W^{2,p}}\leq C\| \psi \| _ p\leq C(\| Lf\| _ p+\| f\| _ p)$. For complex $f$ we apply this to the real and imaginary parts, since $L$ is a real operator.

Step 4: continuous functions with bounded Laplacian. If $f$ is continuous and $Lf$ is bounded, then $f$ and $Lf$ lie in every $L^ p(X)$ with $p<\infty $, which proves the assertion for $1<p<\infty $; the case $p=1$ follows from the case $p=2$, since $X$ has finite volume. Given $0<\alpha <1$, choose $p>d/(1-\alpha )$. In every chart, $f$ belongs to $W^{2,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ by (16.2), so Morrey's inequality (lemma 6.2.6) gives a representative of $f$ in $C^{1,1-d/p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$, hence in $C^{1,\alpha }_{\mathrm{loc}}$. This representative agrees with the continuous function $f$ almost everywhere, hence everywhere. $\square$

Consequently the Hessian forms of obstacles with bounded metric Laplacian can be multiplied on all of $X$, the products being taken pointwise almost everywhere.

Corollary 16.2.12. Let $\theta _1,\ldots ,\theta _ n$ be closed smooth real $(1,1)$-forms on $X$, and let $f_1,\ldots ,f_ n$ be continuous real functions on $X$ with bounded metric Laplacian. Then each $\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j$ is a real $(1,1)$-form whose coefficients, computed from the weak second derivatives of $f_ j$, lie in $L^ p$ for every $p<\infty $. Their pointwise wedge product is defined almost everywhere and satisfies

\[ \bigwedge _{j=1}^ n(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j) =F\, \mathrm dV,\qquad F\in L^ q(X)\quad \text{for every }1\leq q<\infty ; \]

in particular it is a finite signed Radon measure. On a coordinate ball, with smooth real $\rho _ j$ such that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho _ j=\theta _ j$, it equals $n!\pi ^{-n}D(B_1,\ldots ,B_ n)\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$, where $B_ j=((\rho _ j+f_ j)_{a\bar b})_{a,b}$. If moreover $u_ j$ are continuous $\theta _ j$-psh functions on $X$ with bounded metric Laplacian, then on $E=\bigcap _ j\{ u_ j=f_ j\} $ this signed measure agrees with the positive measure $\mathbf1_ E\bigwedge _ j(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)$ of proposition 16.2.9.

Proof. By proposition 16.2.11 and (16.2), every $f_ j$ belongs to $W^{2,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ in every chart, for every finite $p$. In a chart, the current $\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j$ is therefore given by integration against the form $\theta _ j+\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\sum _{a,b}(f_ j)_{a\bar b}\, \mathrm dz_ a\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ b$, where the $(f_ j)_{a\bar b}$ are the weak derivatives, as in (7.5); its coefficients lie in $L^ p_{\mathrm{loc}}$. On the overlap of two charts, the forms obtained in the two charts represent the same current. For a current with locally integrable coefficients, (7.8), in which $\delta $ is the Jacobian determinant relating the two Lebesgue measures, says that the coefficients in the two charts are related, as distributions and hence almost everywhere (proposition 1.4.3(4)), by the pointwise change of variables of forms. Thus the two forms agree almost everywhere as pointwise forms. Hence the pointwise wedge product of the forms $\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j$ is a well-defined $(n,n)$-form, almost everywhere on $X$, and we write it as $F\, \mathrm dV$.

A coefficient of the wedge product in a chart is a sum of products of $n$ coefficients of the factors and of smooth functions; by Hölder's inequality, with each factor in $L^{nq}$ on compact subsets of the chart, these products lie in $L^ q$ there, for every $1\leq q<\infty $. Covering $X$ by finitely many compact subsets of charts, we obtain $F\in L^ q(X)$. A real function in $L^1(X)$ defines a finite signed Radon measure through its positive and negative parts.

On a coordinate ball, $\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\rho _ j+f_ j)=\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\sum _{a,b}(B_ j)_{ab}\, \mathrm dz_ a\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ b$ almost everywhere, and the identity $\omega _{A_1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{A_ n}=D(A_1,\ldots ,A_ n)\, \omega _ I^ n$ of lemma 7.2.2(2), applied pointwise with $A_ j=B_ j/(2\pi )$, together with $\omega _ I^ n=n!\, 2^ n\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$, gives $\bigwedge _ j(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j)=n!\pi ^{-n}D(B_1,\ldots ,B_ n)\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$. Finally, on every coordinate ball $W$ the second identity of proposition 16.2.9, applied with $U=X$, shows that $\mathbf1_ E\bigwedge _ j(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)=\mathbf1_ EF\, \mathrm dV$ on $W$. Since coordinate balls cover $X$, the two measures agree on $X$. $\square$

16.2.3 Penalized equations and metric-Laplacian envelopes

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

We now show that, for a Kähler background, the envelope of the minimum of finitely many obstacles with bounded metric Laplacian again has bounded metric Laplacian (theorem 16.2.24). Throughout this subsection $\kappa $ is a fixed Kähler form on $X$. It is the background of the envelopes, while $\omega $ remains the metric with respect to which the Laplacians of obstacles are measured. Constants are $\kappa $-psh, so for a continuous obstacle $f$ the envelope $P_\kappa (f)$ is a bounded $\kappa $-psh function with $\min _ Xf\leq P_\kappa (f)\leq f$ everywhere, by proposition 16.1.4(1),(2). For smooth $f$ we approximate $P_\kappa (f)$ by the smooth solutions $a_\beta $ of the Monge–Ampère equation with exponential penalty

\[ (\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a)^ n=\mathrm e^{\beta (a-f)}\kappa ^ n, \]

where $\beta >0$ is a large parameter. On the set where $a>f$ the right side is very large, which forces the solution to lie essentially below $f$; on the set where $a<f$ it is very small, just as the Monge–Ampère measure of the envelope vanishes off the contact set. As $\beta \to \infty $ the solutions converge uniformly to $P_\kappa (f)$, with an explicit rate (proposition 16.2.22).

The main estimate is a bound for the Laplacian of $a_\beta $ that is uniform in $\beta $ and depends on the obstacle $f$ only through its supremum norm and an upper bound for the trace $\Lambda \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f=-Lf/(4\pi )$ of its complex Hessian, that is, a lower bound for its metric Laplacian (lemma 16.2.15). This one-sided dependence is what makes finite minima accessible. The complex Hessian of $\min _ jf_ j$ can have a negative singular part on the set where two of the $f_ j$ cross. For instance, in a chart, with $x_1=\operatorname{Re}z_1$, the function $\min \{ x_1,-x_1\} =-|x_1|$ satisfies $\partial _{z_1}\partial _{\bar z_1}(-|x_1|)=-\frac14\partial _{x_1}^2|x_1|=-\frac12\sigma $, where $\sigma $ is Lebesgue measure on the hyperplane $\{ x_1=0\} $. However, the smooth approximations $-\delta \log \sum _ j\mathrm e^{-f_ j/\delta }$ of the minimum have complex Hessians whose traces are bounded above independently of $\delta $ (lemma 16.2.23).

We solve the penalized equation by the continuity method (theorem 16.2.21). Besides the trace bound, the a priori estimates require control of the third derivatives of the solutions. We obtain it from Calabi's maximum principle for the squared norm of the difference of two Chern connections (lemma 16.2.17). The higher derivatives, and the smoothness of the solutions produced by perturbation, are then controlled by the elliptic $L^ p$ estimates of section 16.2.2 (lemma 16.2.19).

In local holomorphic coordinates we put $\partial _ i=\partial _{z_ i}$ and $\partial _{\bar j}=\partial _{\bar z_ j}$, sum over repeated indices, and write

\[ \omega =\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }h_{i\bar j}\, \mathrm dz_ i\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j, \qquad \kappa =\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }k_{i\bar j}\, \mathrm dz_ i\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j, \]

so that $h=(h_{i\bar j})$ and $k=(k_{i\bar j})$ are the coefficient matrices of $2\pi \omega $ and $2\pi \kappa $. As in (14.14), let $(h^{i\bar j})$ be the inverse matrix of $h$, normalized by $h^{i\bar j}h_{l\bar j}=\delta _{il}$ (then also $h^{j\bar i}h_{j\bar l}=\delta _{il}$), and for a Hermitian matrix $m=(m_{i\bar j})$ put $\operatorname {tr}_ hm=h^{i\bar j}m_{i\bar j}$. For a real function $a$ write $a_{i\bar j}=\partial _ i\partial _{\bar j}a$. The operator $D_ h=h^{i\bar j}\partial _ i\partial _{\bar j}$ is the operator $D_ g$ of (14.14) for the Kähler form $\omega '=\omega $, so that

\[ D_ ha=h^{i\bar j}a_{i\bar j}=\Lambda \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a=-\frac{La}{4\pi }. \]

Hence $D_ h$ is the metric Laplacian up to the factor $-1/(4\pi )$; in particular, an upper bound for $D_ hf$ is a lower bound for $Lf$. Since $\omega $ is an arbitrary Kähler form on $X$, everything in this subsection holds with $\omega $ replaced by any Kähler form, for instance by $\kappa $. As $X$ is compact, there is $\gamma >0$ with $k\geq \gamma h$.

For a smooth real function $a$ put $g_{i\bar j}=k_{i\bar j}+a_{i\bar j}$, so that $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a=\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }g_{i\bar j}\, \mathrm dz_ i\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j$. When $g>0$, that is, when $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a$ is a Kähler form, we define $D_ g=g^{i\bar j}\partial _ i\partial _{\bar j}$ and $\operatorname {tr}_ gm=g^{i\bar j}m_{i\bar j}$ in the same way; by (14.14), $D_ g=-L_{\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a}/(4\pi )$. For a $(1,0)$-form $\xi =\xi _ i\, \mathrm dz_ i$ we write $|\xi |_ g^2=g^{i\bar j}\xi _ i\bar\xi _ j$ and $|\xi |_ h^2=h^{i\bar j}\xi _ i\bar\xi _ j$, and we set

\[ H=\operatorname {tr}_ hg=\operatorname {tr}_ hk+D_ ha. \]

The eigenvalues of $g$ relative to $h$ are the eigenvalues of the matrix $h^{-1}g$; they are positive, their sum is $H$, the sum of their inverses is $\operatorname {tr}_ gh$, and their product is $\det g/\det h$.

Under a holomorphic change of coordinates $z=z(w)$ with Jacobian matrix $J=(\partial z_ a/\partial w_ b)$, the coefficient matrices change by $k\mapsto J^{\mathrm T}k\bar J$, so $\det k$ is multiplied by $|\det J|^2$, and $\log \det k$ changes by the function $\log |\det J|^2$. This function is pluriharmonic: for a nonvanishing holomorphic function $F$, $\partial _{\bar j}\log |F|^2=\overline{\partial _ jF}/\bar F$ is antiholomorphic, so $\partial _ i\partial _{\bar j}\log |F|^2=0$. Consequently the ratios $\det k/\det h$ and $\det g/\det k$ are smooth positive functions on $X$, the functions $D_ h\log \det k$ and $D_ h\log \det g$ do not depend on the coordinates, and the functions $\operatorname {Ric}(k)_{i\bar j}=-\partial _ i\partial _{\bar j}\log \det k$ are the coefficients of a well-defined smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\operatorname {Ric}(k)_{i\bar j}\, \mathrm dz_ i\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j$ on $X$.
