Finally, by (16.37), $|f(F(x,r\zeta ))-f(x+r\zeta )|\leq Cr^3\| \nabla f\| _\infty $ for $|\zeta |\leq 1$, and $h(0)=I$, so $|\Psi _ f(x,r)-\Psi ^{\mathrm e}_ f(r)|\leq Cr^3\| \nabla f\| _\infty $. This proves (16.34).

Finally we prove (16.35). We work in a fixed coordinate chart with a smooth frame of $T X$ that is unitary for $h$, obtained by the Gram–Schmidt process, and we describe a tangent vector by its vector $\zeta \in \mathbb C^ n$ of coefficients in this frame. Then $\mathrm d\zeta _ h=\mathrm d\zeta $, and the frame matrix $S(x)$, which satisfies $SS^*=(h^{i\bar j})$, gives the vector $S(x)\zeta $ of coordinate components. In the chart, (16.36) shows that $F(x,\zeta )$ differs by $O(|\zeta |^{N+1})$ from $J_ z(S(x)\zeta )$, where $z$ is the coordinate of $x$ and $J_ z(\xi )=z+\xi -\frac12\Gamma (z)(\xi ,\xi )+O(|\xi |^3)$ is the jet map of the chart. Hence $F(x,\zeta )=x+S(x)\zeta +Q_ x(S(x)\zeta ,S(x)\zeta )+O(|\zeta |^3)$ with the holomorphic quadratic map $Q_ x=-\frac12\Gamma (z)$.

The first moments of the radial weight vanish, and so do the moments $\int \zeta _ i\zeta _ j\chi (|\zeta |^2)\, \mathrm d\zeta $, by the substitution $\zeta \mapsto \mathrm e^{\mathrm it}\zeta $; by symmetry, $\int \zeta _ i\bar\zeta _ j\chi (|\zeta |^2)\, \mathrm d\zeta =a_\chi \delta _{ij}$. For smooth $g$, the second-order Taylor expansion of $g(F(x,r\zeta ))$ in $r\zeta $, integrated against $\chi (|\zeta |^2)\, \mathrm d\zeta $, therefore gives

\[ \Psi _ g(x,r)=g(x)+a_\chi r^2\sum _{i,j}h^{i\bar j}g_{i\bar j}(x)+O(r^3), \]

uniformly on compact subsets of the chart.

For the distributional statement let $\varphi $ be a test function in the chart. For fixed $\zeta $ near $0$ the map $\Phi _\zeta (x)=F(x,\zeta )$ is a diffeomorphism near $\operatorname{Supp}\varphi $, with $\Phi _0$ the identity, and its inverse depends smoothly on $(\zeta ,y)$, by the inverse function theorem. By Fubini's theorem and the change of variables $y=\Phi _{r\zeta }(x)$, the adjoint kernel is

\begin{gather*} \int \Psi _ g(x,r)\varphi (x)\, \mathrm dx=\int g(y)\, \Psi _ r^*\varphi (y)\, \mathrm dy,\\ \Psi _ r^*\varphi (y)=\int \varphi \bigl (\Phi _{r\zeta }^{-1}(y)\bigr )\bigl |\det D\Phi _{r\zeta }^{-1}(y)\bigr |\chi (|\zeta |^2)\, \mathrm d\zeta . \end{gather*}

The integrand is a smooth function of $(r\zeta ,y)$, equal to $\varphi (y)$ at $r=0$, so Taylor's formula in $r\zeta $ gives $\Psi _ r^*\varphi =\varphi +r\varphi _1+r^2\varphi _2+O(r^3)$, with continuous functions $\varphi _1,\varphi _2$ supported in a fixed compact set and a remainder bounded uniformly in $y$. Pairing this with an arbitrary smooth $g$ and comparing with the expansion of $\Psi _ g$, we find $\int g\varphi _1=0$ and $\int g\varphi _2=a_\chi \int \varphi \sum h^{i\bar j}g_{i\bar j}$ for every smooth $g$; hence $\varphi _1=0$ and $\varphi _2=a_\chi \sum _{i,j}\partial _ i\partial _{\bar j}(h^{i\bar j}\varphi )$. Pairing the expansion of $\Psi _ r^*\varphi $ with a locally integrable $u$ gives

\[ \int \frac{\Psi _ u(\cdot ,r)-u}{r^2}\varphi \, \mathrm dx=\int u\, \frac{\Psi _ r^*\varphi -\varphi }{r^2}\, \mathrm dx\longrightarrow a_\chi \int u\sum _{i,j}\partial _ i\partial _{\bar j}(h^{i\bar j}\varphi )\, \mathrm dx, \]

which is (16.35), because the right side is $a_\chi \langle D_ hu,\varphi \rangle $ by the definition of distributional derivatives. $\square$

16.2.6 A minimum with a varying upper radius

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

Near the pole set of the potential of a Kähler current with analytic singularities the smoothing radius must be taken smaller, so the minimum over radii will be taken over an interval whose upper end depends on the base point. Kiselman's minimum principle (theorem 8.5.1) concerns functions that are plurisubharmonic on a domain with open fibers. Here the minimum is taken over a closed interval and may be attained at its end point, and the joint Levi form is controlled only where the slope in the radius is small; the curvature of the end point function then enters. The following lemma records this curvature error explicitly.

Lemma 16.2.28. Let $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a domain, let $\widehat\omega $ be a Hermitian form on $D$, and let $b$ be a smooth real function on $D$ with $\mathrm i\partial \bar\partial b\leq C_ b\widehat\omega $ for a constant $C_ b\geq 0$. Let $H(z,s)$ be a smooth function, defined for $z\in D$ and $s$ in a neighborhood of $(-\infty ,b(z)]$, such that $H_ s\geq 0$ and $H_{ss}>0$, and such that $H$ is bounded below, locally uniformly in $z$, as $s\to -\infty $. Regard $H$ as a function of $(z,\zeta )\in D\times \mathbb C$ with $s=\operatorname{Re}\zeta $.

Fix $c>0$ and a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\Lambda $ on $D$, and let $\pi _ D$ denote the projection to $D$. Suppose that at every point with $s\leq b(z)$ and $H_ s\leq c$ the joint Levi form satisfies

\[ \mathrm i\partial \bar\partial _{z,\zeta }H\geq -\pi _ D^*\Lambda . \]

Then the constrained minimum

\[ v(z)=\inf _{s\leq b(z)}\bigl (H(z,s)-cs\bigr ) \]

is finite and continuous, and it satisfies, in the sense of distributions,

\[ \mathrm i\partial \bar\partial v\geq -\Lambda -cC_ b\widehat\omega . \]

In particular, if $b$ is constant, or more generally plurisuperharmonic, we may take $C_ b=0$, and the upper end point produces no additional curvature error.

Proof. For $\varepsilon >0$ we minimize instead the penalized function

\[ H_\varepsilon (z,s)=H(z,s)-cs-\varepsilon \log (b(z)-s),\qquad s<b(z). \]

For fixed $z$ it tends to $+\infty $ at both ends of the interval: as $s\to -\infty $ the linear term $-cs$ dominates the logarithm, because $H$ is bounded below. Its second derivative in $s$ is $H_{ss}+\varepsilon /(b-s)^2>0$, so it has a unique minimizer $s_\varepsilon (z)$, a nondegenerate critical point. By the implicit function theorem, $s_\varepsilon $ and the minimum value $v_\varepsilon (z)=H_\varepsilon (z,s_\varepsilon (z))$ are smooth. The critical equation is

\[ H_ s-c+\frac\varepsilon {b-s}=0, \]

so at the minimizer $0\leq H_ s\leq c$ and $0\leq \sigma :=\varepsilon /(b-s)\leq c$. Since $\operatorname{Re}\zeta $ is pluriharmonic, the joint Levi form of the barrier $-\varepsilon \log (b(z)-\operatorname{Re}\zeta )$ is $\varepsilon \, \mathrm i\partial \phi \wedge \bar\partial \phi /\phi ^2-\sigma \, \mathrm i\partial \bar\partial b$, where $\phi =b(z)-\operatorname{Re}\zeta $; the first term is nonnegative, and the second is at least $-\sigma C_ b\widehat\omega \geq -cC_ b\widehat\omega $. Hence, at the minimizer, the joint Levi form of $H_\varepsilon $ is bounded below by $-\pi _ D^*(\Lambda +cC_ b\widehat\omega )$.

We now use the Schur complement argument of Kiselman's minimum principle (theorem 8.5.1); it needs the Levi bound only at the minimizer, not elsewhere in the fiber. At the minimizer write the joint complex Hessian of $H_\varepsilon $ in block form $\bigl (\begin{smallmatrix}P& y/2\\ y^*/2& a/4\end{smallmatrix}\bigr )$, where $P=(\partial ^2H_\varepsilon /\partial z_ j\partial \bar z_ k)$, $y=(\partial ^2H_\varepsilon /\partial z_ j\partial s)$ and $a=\partial ^2H_\varepsilon /\partial s^2>0$; here we used that $\partial _\zeta =\partial _{\bar\zeta }=\frac12\partial _ s$ on functions of $z$ and $s=\operatorname{Re}\zeta $. Since $\partial _ sH_\varepsilon (z,s_\varepsilon (z))=0$, the chain rule gives $\partial _{z_ j}v_\varepsilon (z)=(\partial _{z_ j}H_\varepsilon )(z,s_\varepsilon (z))$, and differentiating the critical equation in $\bar z_ k$ gives $\bar y_ k+a\, \partial _{\bar z_ k}s_\varepsilon =0$, because $H_\varepsilon $ is real. Hence $\partial _{z_ j}\partial _{\bar z_ k}v_\varepsilon =P_{jk}+y_ j\partial _{\bar z_ k}s_\varepsilon $, that is, the complex Hessian of $v_\varepsilon $ is $P-a^{-1}yy^*$.

To bound it from below, let $M$ be the coefficient matrix of $\Lambda +cC_ b\widehat\omega =\mathrm i\sum _{j,k}M_{jk}\, \mathrm dz_ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ k$. By the Levi bound at the minimizer, the block matrix $\bigl (\begin{smallmatrix}P+M& y/2\\ y^*/2& a/4\end{smallmatrix}\bigr )$ is positive semidefinite, and evaluating its Hermitian form at the vector $(\xi ,-2a^{-1}y^*\xi )$, $\xi \in \mathbb C^ n$, gives $\xi ^*(P+M-a^{-1}yy^*)\xi \geq 0$. Thus

\[ \mathrm i\partial \bar\partial v_\varepsilon \geq -\Lambda -cC_ b\widehat\omega . \]

It remains to pass to the limit $\varepsilon \to 0$. On a compact neighborhood in the base, $b$ is bounded, and evaluating at $s=b-1$ gives a uniform upper bound for $v_\varepsilon $ when $0<\varepsilon \leq 1$. Since $H$ is bounded below and $-cs-\log (b-s)$ grows linearly as $s\to -\infty $, the minimizers $s_\varepsilon $ are bounded below uniformly; the minimizers of the unpenalized problem have the same lower bound. Both problems may therefore be restricted to a common compact interval below their varying upper end point. The unpenalized minimum $v$ is finite, and it is continuous: after the substitution $s=b(z)-t$ the upper end point becomes fixed, the range of $t$ is a fixed compact interval, and a minimum of a continuous function over a fixed compact interval depends continuously on the parameter.

On this range the penalty is bounded below by a quantity that tends uniformly to zero, so $v_\varepsilon \geq v-o(1)$. For an upper bound, evaluate $H_\varepsilon $ at an unpenalized minimizer if it lies at least $\sqrt\varepsilon $ below $b$, and otherwise at $s=b-\sqrt\varepsilon $. In the first case the penalty is at most $\varepsilon |\log \sqrt\varepsilon |$; in the second, the unpenalized function changes by $o(1)$, uniformly, by its uniform continuity on the compact range. Consequently $v_\varepsilon \to v$ locally uniformly, and the inequalities for $\mathrm i\partial \bar\partial v_\varepsilon $ pass to the distributional limit. $\square$

16.2.7 Big-class envelopes on the ample locus

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

We now prove that envelopes in a big class have locally bounded metric Laplacian away from the non-Kähler locus. The positivity that $\theta $ lacks is supplied by a Kähler current in $\{ \theta \} $ with analytic singularities, whose pole set becomes a divisor after a modification. Near that divisor we shrink the smoothing radius of lemma 16.2.27. A first bound for the admissible radius comes from the maximality of the envelope. High powers of the local equations of the divisor then turn the variable coefficients in the joint Hessian estimate into uniform ones, and a second application of maximality gives a quadratic bound for the smoothing error.

Theorem 16.2.29. Suppose that $\{ \theta \} $ is big, let $r\geq 1$ be an integer, and let $f_1,\ldots ,f_ r$ be continuous real functions on $X$ with bounded metric Laplacian. Let

\[ \operatorname {Amp}(\{ \theta \} )=X\setminus E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\{ \theta \} ) \]

be the ample locus of $\{ \theta \} $, the complement of its non-Kähler locus (definition 14.4.1). Then

\[ u=P_\theta \bigl (\min _ j f_ j\bigr ) \]

has bounded metric Laplacian on every compact subset of $\operatorname {Amp}(\{ \theta \} )$. On $\operatorname {Amp}(\{ \theta \} )$ its complex Hessian has locally bounded coefficients, $u\in W^{2,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every $1\leq p<\infty $, and hence $u\in C^{1,\gamma }_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every $0<\gamma <1$.

Proof. After reducing to one obstacle and passing to a modification, we bound the smoothing error $H_ u(x,r)-u(x)$ off the divisor, first linearly and then quadratically in $r$, as outlined above; the quadratic bound and (16.35) then bound $D_ hu$, and the Sobolev regularity follows.

Reduction to one obstacle with bounded complex Hessian. Choose a Kähler form $\kappa \geq \theta $, for instance a large multiple of $\omega $, and put $f=P_\kappa (\min _ jf_ j)$. By theorem 16.2.24, $f$ is $C^1$ and has bounded complex Hessian. Every $\theta $-psh function below $\min _ jf_ j$ is $\kappa $-psh, and hence lies below $f$. Conversely $f\leq \min _ jf_ j$. These two inclusions of competitor families give $P_\theta (f)=u$.

Passage to a modification. Put $Z=E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\{ \theta \} )$. By theorem 14.4.4, $Z$ is the pole set of a function $\phi _1\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with analytic singularities such that $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _1\geq 2\delta _1\omega $ for some $\delta _1>0$. We apply lemma 13.4.1 to $\phi _1$, regarded as an element of $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta -2\delta _1\omega )$, with an integer $m_0$ so large that the constant $c'$ of that lemma satisfies $c'/m_0\leq \delta _1$, and we let $\phi _0$ be the resulting function. Then $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _0\geq \delta _1\omega $, and $\phi _0$ has gentle analytic singularities (definition 13.1.11) of type $(m_0^{-1},\mathfrak a)$, where $\mathfrak a=\mathcal I(m_0\phi _1)$. The pole set of $\phi _0$ is the zero set of $\mathfrak a$, and it equals $Z$. Indeed, near a point where $\phi _1$ is finite, $\phi _1$ is bounded, because it has analytic singularities, so $\mathfrak a$ is the unit ideal there; thus the pole set of $\phi _0$ is contained in $Z$. Conversely, $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _0$ is a Kähler current whose potential has analytic singularities, so its pole set contains $Z$ by the definition of the non-Kähler locus (definition 14.4.1).

Let $\pi \colon Y\to X$ be a log resolution of $\mathfrak a$, that is, a principalization of $\mathfrak a$ by finitely many blowups with smooth centers (theorem A.5.5), and write $\pi ^*\mathfrak a=\mathcal O_ Y(-D_0)$; the support of the effective divisor $D_0$ is $\pi ^{-1}(Z)$. By definition 13.1.11, locally on $Y$ the function $\phi _0\circ \pi $ is $m_0^{-1}\log |s_{D_0}|^2$ plus a smooth function. By theorem A.5.5, $\pi $ is an isomorphism over $X\setminus Z$, and all its centers lie over $Z$. If $\sigma $ blows up a compact Kähler manifold with Kähler form $\omega '$ along a closed submanifold of positive codimension, with exceptional divisor $E$, then $\sigma ^*\omega '+\varepsilon \eta $ is Kähler for every sufficiently small $\varepsilon >0$, where $\eta $ is the curvature of a suitable smooth quadratic Hermitian metric on $\mathcal O(-E)$ (proposition 13.1.10). Applying this to the successive blowups of $\pi $, with rational $\varepsilon $, and pulling the forms back to $Y$, we obtain a Kähler form

\[ \omega _ Y=\pi ^*\omega +\sum _\ell \varepsilon _\ell \eta _\ell >0, \]

where $\eta _\ell $ is the curvature of a smooth metric on $\mathcal O(-E_\ell )$ for effective exceptional divisors $E_\ell $, and the coefficients $\varepsilon _\ell >0$ can be taken rational. The pulled-back current $\pi ^*(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _0)$ is at least $\delta _1\pi ^*\omega $. Let $s_{E_\ell }$ be the canonical section of $\mathcal O(E_\ell )$, with norm $|s_{E_\ell }|$ computed in the dual metric. Choose a rational number $0<\delta _2\leq \delta _1$ and add $\delta _2\sum _\ell \varepsilon _\ell \log |s_{E_\ell }|^2$ to the pulled-back potential $\phi _0\circ \pi $. Since, by the Poincaré–Lelong formula (theorem 7.4.5),

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |s_{E_\ell }|^2=[E_\ell ]+\eta _\ell , \]

the resulting current is at least $\delta _2\omega _ Y$. Locally its potential is still of the form $\sum _ j\beta _ j\log |g_ j|^2$ plus a smooth function, where the $g_ j$ are local equations of the components of an effective divisor with support $\pi ^{-1}(Z)$ (the exceptional divisors lie over $Z$) and the $\beta _ j$ are positive rational numbers. Replacing $\omega _ Y$ by $\delta _2\omega _ Y$, we may assume that the lower bound is $\omega _ Y$ itself.

By proposition 14.2.8, pullback is a bijection from $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ onto $\mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$. Since $f$ is continuous, a potential lies below $f$ if and only if its pullback lies below $f\circ \pi $, so $u\circ \pi =P_{\pi ^*\theta }(f\circ \pi )$. The pullback $f\circ \pi $ is $C^1$ and has bounded complex Hessian. Indeed, off $\pi ^{-1}(Z)$ the map $\pi $ is a local biholomorphism, and since its differential is bounded, the chain rule bounds the coefficients of $\pi ^*(\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f)$ there. Locally, $f\circ \pi $ plus a smooth local potential of $\pi ^*\kappa $ is a bounded plurisubharmonic function. Applying theorem 9.3.1 to it and to $\log |g|$, where $g$ is a local equation of the hypersurface $\pi ^{-1}(Z)$, shows that this logarithm is integrable for the trace measure of its complex Hessian, so the trace measure gives $\pi ^{-1}(Z)$ zero mass. Hence the positive current $\pi ^*\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(f\circ \pi )$, whose coefficients are bounded off $\pi ^{-1}(Z)$, has bounded coefficients everywhere. We may therefore work on $Y$ with the pulled-back form and obstacle, and return through the isomorphism over $X\setminus Z$ at the end.

From now on we write $X$, $\theta $, $\omega $, $f$ and $u$ for $Y$, $\pi ^*\theta $, the Kähler form just constructed, $f\circ \pi $ and $u\circ \pi $, and again put $\alpha =2\pi \theta $ and $\widehat\omega =2\pi \omega $. Adding a constant to the obstacle, we may assume that $0\leq f\leq B$ with $B\geq 1$. Let $\phi $ be the potential $\phi _0\circ \pi +\delta _2\sum _\ell \varepsilon _\ell \log |s_{E_\ell }|^2$ constructed above, minus a constant chosen so that

16.55
\begin{equation} \alpha +\mathcal D\phi \geq \widehat\omega ,\qquad \phi \leq -B-1,\qquad \phi \leq u\leq f\leq B; \end{equation}

here $\phi \leq u$ holds because $\phi $ is a competitor for the envelope. Cover $X$ by finitely many coordinate charts, with smaller charts still covering $X$, on each of which

\[ \phi =\sum _ j \beta _ j\log |g_ j|^2+\sigma , \qquad \beta _ j>0,\quad |g_ j|<\tfrac 12, \]

where the $g_ j$ are local equations of the components of the divisor meeting the chart (there are none if the chart does not meet the divisor) and $\sigma $ is smooth. Write $D$ for the divisor and $U=X\setminus D$. In particular $\mathcal D\phi $ is uniformly bounded on $U$, although $\phi $ tends to $-\infty $ near $D$. All bounds below are uniform on this finite atlas; only the explicitly displayed functions of $\phi $ are allowed to grow.

A first-order smoothing bound. Let $H=H_ u$ and $\lambda =\lambda _ u$ be as in lemma 16.2.27. The initial joint estimate (16.32) improves after adding $K_1r$. Indeed, the vertical Levi form of $K_1|w|$ is $K_1/(4r)$, and Young's inequality absorbs the bounded mixed term at a horizontal cost $Cr$. Hence, for a fixed sufficiently large $K_1$ and small $r$,

16.56
\begin{equation} \alpha +\mathcal D(H+K_1r)\geq -(A\lambda +Cr)\widehat\omega \end{equation}

as joint forms; the right side is pulled back from $X$.

Fix a small $\delta _0>0$; it will be decreased finitely many times, each time depending only on the fixed data. For $0<\delta <\delta _0$ and $c>0$ define on $U$

\[ \psi _{c,\delta }(x)=\inf _{0<t\leq \delta } \{ H(x,t)+K_1t-K_1\delta -c\log (t/\delta )\} . \]

The infimum over $t$ is finite and attained, because $H\geq u$, $u$ is locally bounded on $U$, and $-c\log (t/\delta )\to +\infty $ as $t\to 0$. At a minimizing radius, including the upper end point, the logarithmic slope $\lambda +K_1t$ of the nondecreasing part $H+K_1t$ is at most $c$, so $\lambda \leq c$. We apply lemma 16.2.28 in the variable $s=\log t$, with the constant upper end point $\log \delta $, to $H(x,\mathrm e^ s)+K_1\mathrm e^ s$ plus a smooth local potential of $\alpha $; by (16.56), the Levi bound required in the lemma holds with $\Lambda =(Ac+C\delta )\widehat\omega $. Moreover, since $u\leq f$ and the kernel of $\Psi $ is positive, evaluation at $t=\delta $ gives $\psi _{c,\delta }\leq \Psi _ u(x,\delta )+K\delta ^2\leq \Psi _ f(x,\delta )+K\delta ^2$, and (16.34) bounds the right side by $f+C\delta ^2$. Thus

\[ \alpha +\mathcal D\psi _{c,\delta }\geq -(Ac+C\delta )\widehat\omega , \qquad \psi _{c,\delta }\leq f+C\delta ^2. \]

Put $e=Ac+C\delta $ and $\tau =e/(1+e)$. Then $(1-\tau )(\psi _{c,\delta }-C\delta ^2)+\tau \phi $ is $\theta $-psh on $U$, by (16.55), since $-(1-\tau )e+\tau =0$; and it lies below $(1-\tau )f\leq f$, because $\phi \leq 0\leq f$. It is bounded above near $D$, so by theorem 8.4.1, applied after adding smooth local potentials of $\alpha $, it extends across $D$; the extension is given by upper limits and therefore still lies below the continuous obstacle. By the maximality of the envelope, this extension lies below $u$. Rearranging, and using $\psi _{c,\delta }\leq B+C\delta ^2$ so that no unbounded factor is divided by $1-\tau $, we obtain

16.57
\begin{equation} \psi _{c,\delta }\leq u+C\delta ^2 +\tau (B-\phi +C\delta ^2). \end{equation}

Now take $c=\delta $, so that $e$ and $\tau $ are $O(\delta )$, and let $t_0$ be a minimizing radius for $\psi _{\delta ,\delta }(x)$. The two nonnegative quantities $H(x,t_0)+K_1t_0-u(x)$ and $\delta \log (\delta /t_0)$ add up to $\psi _{\delta ,\delta }(x)-u(x)+K_1\delta $, so (16.57) gives, with $R=C(1+B+|\phi |)$ for a suitable uniform $C$,

\[ t_0\geq \delta \mathrm e^{-R(x)},\qquad H(x,t_0)+K_1t_0-u(x)\leq R(x)\delta . \]

Since $H$ is nondecreasing in the radius, the choice $\delta =r\mathrm e^{R(x)}$ yields

16.58
\begin{equation} 0\leq H(x,r)-u(x)\leq R(x)\mathrm e^{R(x)}r \quad \text{if }0<r<\delta _0\mathrm e^{-R(x)}. \end{equation}

Since $\phi \leq -B-1$, we have $1+B\leq |\phi |$, so we may take $R(x)=B_0|\phi (x)|$ with a fixed large $B_0$.

This estimate also improves the bound for the trace mass. Enlarging $B_0$ if necessary, we may assume $B_0\beta _ j\geq 1$ for all $j$; since $|g_ j|<1/2$, the formula for $\phi $ then gives $\mathrm e^{-R(x)}\leq C|g_ j(x)|^2$ for each local equation $g_ j$. As the first derivatives of the $g_ j$ are bounded, there is a fixed small $S>0$ such that, for $|y-x|\leq S\mathrm e^{-R(x)}$, the ratios $|g_ j(y)|/|g_ j(x)|$ lie between $1/2$ and $3/2$. Hence $|\phi (y)-\phi (x)|\leq C$ there. On a chart that does not meet the divisor, $\phi $ is smooth, and the same oscillation bound holds after decreasing $S$. By (16.55), $|u(y)|\leq C(1+|\phi (x)|)$ on that ball. The comparison with radial convolution in lemma 16.2.27 therefore transfers (16.58) to the Euclidean radial mean of the local plurisubharmonic potential $p=u+q$, with an additional error $Cr^2(1+|\phi (x)|)$.

We also need the following consequence of the Jensen–Lelong formula, with positive constants $c_0$ and $0<c_1<1$ independent of $x$ and $r$:

\[ \int p(x+r\zeta )\chi (|\zeta |^2)\, \mathrm d\zeta -p(x) \geq c_0 r^{2-d}\mu _ p(B(x,c_1r)), \qquad d=2n. \]

Indeed, the spherical means of $p$ about $x$ are nondecreasing in the radius and tend to $p(x)$. Letting $r_1\to 0$ in (12.4) and using that $D_ p(x,\cdot )$ is nondecreasing, we see that the spherical mean of radius $\varrho $ exceeds $p(x)$ by at least $2\log (1/c_1)D_ p(x,c_1\varrho )$, which by (12.3) is a fixed positive multiple of $\varrho ^{2-d}\mu _ p(B(x,c_1\varrho ))$. It remains to integrate over the radii $\varrho =r\varrho '$ with $\varrho '$ in a fixed subinterval of $(0,1)$ on which $\chi (\varrho '^2)>0$, and to decrease $c_1$. We apply this inequality at a fixed multiple of $r$, decrease $S$, and use (16.58). This gives

16.59
\begin{equation} m_ u(x,r)\leq \frac{M(x)}r\quad (0<r<S\mathrm e^{-R(x)}), \qquad 1\leq M(x)\leq C R(x)\mathrm e^{CR(x)}. \end{equation}

Thus the improved joint estimate (16.33) is available for these radii.

Uniform joint estimates in divisor coordinates. On each chart put $G=(\prod _ jg_ j)^ m$, where an empty product equals one. By the formula for $R$ and (16.59), $M$ is bounded by a fixed product of negative powers of the $|g_ j|$, and differentiating $G$ loses only one power of one factor. Hence we can choose the integer $m$ so large that $M|G|$ and $M|\mathrm dG|$ are uniformly bounded. Next, by the same formula for $R$, we can choose an integer $k\geq 1$ so large that

\[ \frac{\mathrm e^{-kR(x)}}{|G(x)|}\leq T \]

with $T$ uniform on the finite cover. Put $\rho _\delta (x)=\delta \mathrm e^{-kR(x)}$, and take $\delta _0<S$, so that these radii lie in the range of (16.59). In the holomorphic coordinate $\xi $ defined by $w=G(x)\xi $, the range $0<|w|\leq \rho _\delta (x)$ corresponds to $|\xi |\leq T\delta $.

The differential of the change of variables $(x,\xi )\mapsto (x,G(x)\xi )$ sends $(v,\eta )$ to $(v,G\eta +\xi \, \mathrm dG(v))$. Substituting this in (16.33) and using the bounds just obtained, $|G|\leq 1$ and a uniform bound for $|\mathrm dG|$, we get

16.60
\begin{align} (\alpha +\mathcal D H(x,|G\xi |))[v,\eta ]^2 \geq {}& -(A\lambda +C_1|\xi |^2)|v|^2\notag \\ & -C_1|G||\xi ||v||\eta | -C_1|G|^2|\xi ||\eta |^2. \end{align}

For example, the purely horizontal error is bounded by a constant times $M(|G|^2+|G||\mathrm dG|+|G||\mathrm dG|^2)|\xi |^2$, the mixed coefficient by $C|G||\xi |$, and the vertical coefficient by $C|G|^2|\xi |$. These estimates use only the boundedness of $M|G|$ and $M|\mathrm dG|$.

Now add $K_2|G\xi |^2$. Its Levi form is the square $K_2|G\eta +\xi \, \mathrm dG(v)|^2$, and the inequality $|a+b|^2\geq \tfrac 12|a|^2-|b|^2$ shows that it is at least $\frac{K_2}2|G|^2|\eta |^2$ minus a horizontal term $K_2|\mathrm dG|^2|\xi |^2|v|^2$. For $\delta _0$ small and $K_2$ fixed and large, this absorbs the last term of (16.60), and Young's inequality absorbs its mixed term at a cost $C|\xi |^2|v|^2$. Since $|\xi |\leq T\delta $, we have proved the uniform joint bound

16.61
\begin{equation} \alpha +\mathcal D(H(x,|G\xi |)+K_2|G\xi |^2) \geq -(A\lambda +C_2\delta ^2)\widehat\omega . \end{equation}

All constants here are independent of the chart and of the point of $U$.

A second-order smoothing bound. For $c>0$ set

\[ \psi (x)=\inf _{0<t\leq \rho _\delta (x)} \{ H(x,t)+K_2t^2-c\log t\} . \]

In the variable $s=\log |\xi |$, so that $t=|G|\mathrm e^ s$, the upper end point of the interval is $b(x)=\log \delta -kR(x)-\log |G(x)|$. The last summand is pluriharmonic on $U$, and $\mathcal D R=-B_0\mathcal D\phi $ is uniformly bounded there. Thus $|\mathcal D b|\leq C_ b\widehat\omega $ with a uniform constant $C_ b$. The logarithmic slope of the nondecreasing part $H+K_2t^2$ is $\lambda +2K_2t^2$, so at a minimizing radius it is at most $c$. Since $-c\log t=-cs-c\log |G|$ and $\log |G|$ is pluriharmonic on $U$, lemma 16.2.28, applied to (16.61) (again after adding a smooth local potential of $\alpha $), proves

16.62
\begin{equation} \alpha +\mathcal D\psi \geq -C_0\widehat\omega , \qquad C_0=A'c+C_2\delta ^2,\quad A'=A+C_ b. \end{equation}

Let $a=ckB_0$ and $C_3=C_ f+K+K_2$, with $C_ f$ from (16.34). Since $-c\log \rho _\delta =-c\log \delta -a\phi $, evaluation at $t=\rho _\delta (x)\leq \delta $, together with $\Psi _ u\leq \Psi _ f$ and (16.34), gives

\[ Q:=\psi +c\log \delta +a\phi -C_3\delta ^2\leq f, \qquad (1+a)\alpha +\mathcal DQ\geq -C_0\widehat\omega ; \]

the second inequality follows from (16.62) and $a(\alpha +\mathcal D\phi )\geq 0$, a consequence of (16.55). Put

\[ \nu =\frac{C_0}{1+a+C_0},\qquad W=(1-\nu )Q+\nu (1+a)\phi . \]

Since $\nu (1+a)=(1-\nu )C_0$, we have $(1+a)\alpha +\mathcal DW\geq 0$, that is, $W$ is $(1+a)\theta $-psh. It lies below $f$, because $Q\leq f$, $\phi \leq 0$ and $f\geq 0$. As before, $W$ is bounded above near $D$, so it extends across $D$ by theorem 8.4.1, applied after adding smooth local potentials of $(1+a)\alpha $, and the extension, given by upper limits, still lies below the continuous function $f$. Consequently $W/(1+a)$, which lies below $f/(1+a)\leq f$, is a competitor for $u$, and $(1+a)u\geq W$. Since $u\leq B$ and $Q\leq B$, we can rearrange this without losing an unbounded positive term:

16.63
\begin{equation} u\geq Q+\nu (1+a)\phi -(\nu +a)B. \end{equation}

Now take $c=\delta ^2$, so that $a$ and $\nu $ are $O(\delta ^2)$, and let $t_0$ be a minimizing radius for $\psi (x)$. Writing $\psi (x)$ as the value at $t_0$ of the minimized expression and substituting it into (16.63), we obtain

16.64
\begin{equation} t_0\geq \delta \mathrm e^{-R_2(x)},\qquad 0\leq H(x,t_0)+K_2t_0^2-u(x)\leq R_2(x)\delta ^2, \qquad R_2=C(1+B+|\phi |). \end{equation}

Indeed, after rearrangement the left side consists of the two nonnegative terms $H(x,t_0)+K_2t_0^2-u(x)$ and $-\delta ^2\log (t_0/\delta )$ (recall $H\geq u$ and $t_0\leq \delta $), while every term on the right is bounded by $C\delta ^2(1+B+|\phi |)$.

Fix a compact set $F\Subset U$ and a number $M_2\geq \sup _ FR_2$. For every sufficiently small $r$, choose $\delta =r\mathrm e^{M_2}$. Then $r\leq t_0$ at every $x\in F$, and since $H$ is nondecreasing in the radius we obtain the uniform quadratic estimate

16.65
\begin{equation} 0\leq H(x,r)-u(x)\leq M_2\mathrm e^{2M_2}r^2, \qquad x\in F. \end{equation}

Regularity. Divide (16.65) by $r^2$: on $F$ we have $0\leq (\Psi _ u(x,r)-u(x))/r^2+K\leq M_2\mathrm e^{2M_2}$. Pairing with nonnegative test functions supported in the interior of $F$ and letting $r\to 0$, (16.35) shows that, in the sense of distributions on the interior of $F$, $a_\chi D_ hu+K$ lies between zero and the constant $M_2\mathrm e^{2M_2}$. In particular $|\langle a_\chi D_ hu,\varphi \rangle |\leq (K+M_2\mathrm e^{2M_2})\int \varphi \, \mathrm dx$ for these test functions $\varphi \geq 0$. Since every point of $U$ lies in the interior of such a set $F$, corollary 1.7.2(2), applied in the charts with Lebesgue measure, shows that $D_ hu$ is an $L^\infty _{\mathrm{loc}}$ function on $U$. The positive current $\alpha +\mathcal Du$ therefore has locally bounded $h$-trace, so in a fixed chart each of its diagonal coefficient measures is bounded by a constant times Lebesgue measure. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for positive Hermitian matrices bounds the off-diagonal coefficient measures as well. Thus all complex coefficients of $\alpha +\mathcal Du$, and hence those of $\mathcal Du$, are locally bounded on $U$.

It remains to obtain the real Sobolev regularity; note that no weak first derivatives of $u$ are known in advance. Let $V\Subset U$ be a coordinate ball. The complex second derivatives $u_{i\bar j}$ are bounded on $V$, so the Euclidean Laplacian $\Delta u=4\sum _ iu_{i\bar i}$ is a bounded function there. Let $V'$ be a smaller concentric ball with $\overline{V'}\subset V$, choose $\vartheta \in C^\infty _ c(V)$ equal to one near $\overline{V'}$, and let $N=E_{2n}*g$ be the fundamental potential of the bounded compactly supported function $g=\vartheta \Delta u$, where $E_{2n}$ is the fundamental solution of proposition 2.2.1. By proposition 3.2.1, applied to the positive and negative parts of $g\, \mathrm dx$, we have $\Delta N=g$ in the sense of distributions. Hence $u-N$ is distributionally harmonic near $\overline{V'}$, and by Weyl's lemma (theorem 2.1.4) it is smooth there. It therefore suffices to show that $N\in W^{2,p}(V')$ for $1<p<\infty $.

The Hessian estimate of theorem 6.2.3 concerns compactly supported smooth functions, so we mollify. Let $g_\varepsilon $ be the mollifications of $g$, and put $N_\varepsilon =E_{2n}*g_\varepsilon $. For small $\varepsilon $ the $g_\varepsilon $ are smooth and supported in a fixed compact set, and $g_\varepsilon \to g$ in $L^ s$ for every $1\leq s<\infty $, by lemma 1.4.1. By proposition 2.2.1, $N_\varepsilon $ is smooth and $\Delta N_\varepsilon =g_\varepsilon $. Moreover $\partial _ a N_\varepsilon =E_{2n}*\partial _ ag_\varepsilon =(\partial _ aE_{2n})*g_\varepsilon $, where the second identity follows by integration by parts outside a small ball about the singularity of $E_{2n}$, whose boundary term tends to zero. Fix $q_0$ with $1<q_0<2n/(2n-1)$, and let $q_0'=q_0/(q_0-1)$. The functions $E_{2n}$ and $\partial _ aE_{2n}$, the latter bounded by $C|y|^{1-2n}$, belong to $L^{q_0}$ on bounded sets, so Hölder's inequality gives

\[ \sup _ V|N_\varepsilon -N|+\sup _ V\bigl |\partial _ aN_\varepsilon -(\partial _ aE_{2n})*g\bigr | \leq C\| g_\varepsilon -g\| _{L^{q_0'}}\longrightarrow 0. \]

Thus $N_\varepsilon \to N$ and $\partial _ aN_\varepsilon \to (\partial _ aE_{2n})*g$ uniformly on $V$. Passing to the limit in the sense of distributions, we see that $N$ is bounded on $V$ and that its first distributional derivatives there are the bounded functions $(\partial _ aE_{2n})*g$. Choose $\vartheta _1\in C^\infty _ c(V)$ equal to one on $V'$. For small $\varepsilon ,\varepsilon '$, the function $\vartheta _1(N_\varepsilon -N_{\varepsilon '})$ is smooth with compact support, and (6.7) gives

\[ \bigl \| D^2\bigl (\vartheta _1(N_\varepsilon -N_{\varepsilon '})\bigr )\bigr \| _ p \leq C_{2n,p}\bigl \| \vartheta _1(g_\varepsilon -g_{\varepsilon '}) +2\nabla \vartheta _1\cdot \nabla (N_\varepsilon -N_{\varepsilon '}) +(N_\varepsilon -N_{\varepsilon '})\Delta \vartheta _1\bigr \| _ p. \]

By the convergences just proved, the right side tends to zero as $\varepsilon ,\varepsilon '\to 0$. Hence, along a sequence $\varepsilon _ k\to 0$, the second derivatives $\partial _ a\partial _ b(\vartheta _1N_{\varepsilon _ k})$ form a Cauchy sequence in $L^ p(\mathbb R^{2n})$ and converge in $L^ p$. The limits are supported in the fixed compact set $\operatorname{Supp}\vartheta _1$, so by Hölder's inequality the convergence also holds in the sense of distributions. On the other hand $\vartheta _1N_{\varepsilon _ k}\to \vartheta _1N$ uniformly, so $\partial _ a\partial _ b(\vartheta _1N_{\varepsilon _ k})\to \partial _ a\partial _ b(\vartheta _1N)$ in the sense of distributions. Therefore $\partial _ a\partial _ b(\vartheta _1N)$ is the $L^ p$ limit and belongs to $L^ p$, and since $\vartheta _1=1$ on $V'$, the second distributional derivatives of $N$ belong to $L^ p(V')$.

Hence $N\in W^{2,p}(V')$, and therefore $u\in W^{2,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$ for every $1<p<\infty $; the case $p=1$ follows on relatively compact sets because their volume is finite. Morrey's inequality (lemma 6.2.6), with $p>2n/(1-\gamma )$, gives a representative in $C^{1,\gamma }_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every $\gamma <1$. This continuous representative coincides with $u$: by lemma 13.1.4(1), the quasi-plurisubharmonic function $u$ is at every point the limit of its averages over small coordinate balls centered there, and these averages converge to the continuous representative. Transferring these statements through the biholomorphism $Y\setminus \pi ^{-1}(Z)\to X\setminus Z$, under which all smooth metrics are locally equivalent and local Sobolev spaces are preserved (lemma 6.2.4(2)), proves the theorem. $\square$

16.2.8 Contact with an arbitrary singular potential

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

Proposition 16.2.9 computes the contact density of a potential with bounded metric Laplacian. We now remove this regularity assumption on the potential and keep it only for the obstacle. Let $u$ be an arbitrary quasi-plurisubharmonic potential below an obstacle $f$ with bounded metric Laplacian. Between $u$ and $f$ we insert the envelopes of smooth functions decreasing to $u$; by theorem 16.2.24 they have bounded metric Laplacian, so the regular theory applies to their contact with $f$. We then pass to the limit. The non-pluripolar measures of a decreasing sequence of unbounded potentials need not converge, so before passing to the limit we truncate at a fixed level and multiply by a bounded weight that vanishes below this level. The smooth functions decreasing to $u$ are provided by the following lemma.

Lemma 16.2.30. Let $u\colon X\to [-\infty ,\infty )$ be upper semicontinuous. Then $u$ is the pointwise limit of a decreasing sequence of smooth real functions $\phi _ j$ on $X$.

Proof. If $u$ is identically $-\infty $, the constants $\phi _ j=-j$ will do, so we assume that $u$ takes a finite value somewhere. Fix a distance $d$ defining the topology of the compact manifold $X$, and put $M=\max _ Xu<\infty $. For each positive integer $j$ define

\[ v_ j(x)=\max _{y\in X}\{ u(y)-j d(x,y)\} ; \]

the maximum is attained because the function in braces is upper semicontinuous in $y$. The $v_ j$ are finite and $j$-Lipschitz: a point where $u$ is finite bounds the maximum from below, and the triangle inequality bounds their differences. They decrease in $j$ and dominate $u$ (take $y=x$). If $u(x)$ is finite, a maximizing point $y_ j$ satisfies $jd(x,y_ j)\leq M-u(x)$, so $y_ j\to x$, and since $v_ j(x)\leq u(y_ j)$, upper semicontinuity gives $v_ j(x)\downarrow u(x)$. If $u(x)=-\infty $, then for every $N$ there is a small ball about $x$ on which $u<-N$, while outside this ball $u(y)-jd(x,y)\leq M-jd(x,y)<-N$ for large $j$. Thus $v_ j(x)\downarrow u(x)$ in this case as well.

The $v_ j$ are only Lipschitz, so we smooth them while keeping monotonicity. Set $\phi _0=M+1$ and $\eta _ j=4^{-j}$, and suppose inductively that $\phi _{j-1}\geq u+\eta _{j-1}$. The continuous function

\[ q_ j=\min \{ \phi _{j-1}-2\eta _ j,\ v_ j+2\eta _ j\} \]

is at least $u+2\eta _ j$, since $\eta _{j-1}=4\eta _ j$. A continuous function on $X$ can be approximated uniformly by smooth functions: convolution in a chart converges uniformly on compact subsets of the chart (lemma 1.4.1(1)), and a finite smooth partition of unity subordinate to charts (proposition 7.1.3) sums these errors with total weight one. Choose a smooth real $\phi _ j$ with $\| \phi _ j-q_ j\| _\infty <\eta _ j$. Then

\[ u+\eta _ j\leq \phi _ j\leq \phi _{j-1}-\eta _ j, \qquad \phi _ j\leq v_ j+3\eta _ j. \]

These bounds continue the induction, show that the sequence $\phi _ j$ decreases, and, since $v_ j+3\eta _ j\downarrow u$, show that $\phi _ j\downarrow u$, also at the points where $u=-\infty $. $\square$

Theorem 16.2.31. Let $\theta _1,\ldots ,\theta _ n$ be closed smooth real $(1,1)$-forms. For each $j$ let $u_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$, and let $f_ j$ be a continuous real function on $X$ with bounded metric Laplacian. Suppose that $u_ j\leq f_ j$ everywhere, and put $E=\bigcap _ j\{ u_ j=f_ j\} $. Then

16.66
\begin{equation} \mathbf1_ E\left\langle \bigwedge _{j=1}^ n (\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)\right\rangle =\mathbf1_ E\bigwedge _{j=1}^ n(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j). \end{equation}

Here the right side is the restriction to $E$ of the finite signed measure of corollary 16.2.12, defined by the weak second derivatives of the $f_ j$; on $E$ its density is nonnegative and bounded.

Proof. We first prove the scalar case, in which all $\theta _ j$, $u_ j$ and $f_ j$ coincide, in three steps of increasing generality (Steps 1–3). The mixed case then follows by polarization (Step 4), and Step 5 proves the assertion about the density.

Step 1: a Kähler background and a plurisubharmonic obstacle. Let $\kappa $ be a Kähler form, let $u$ be $\kappa $-psh, and let $f\geq u$ be a continuous $\kappa $-psh function with bounded metric Laplacian. Write $F=\{ u=f\} $ and $\mu _ a=\langle (\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a)^ n\rangle $ for $\kappa $-psh $a$. Since $f$ is bounded, $\mu _ f$ is the Bedford–Taylor product $(\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f)^ n$ (proposition 15.3.3, applied with smooth local potentials of $\kappa $). By proposition 16.2.9, applied with $u_ j=f_ j=f$, this measure is given by the weak second derivatives of $f$, so it is the measure $(\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f)^ n$ of corollary 16.2.12; we must show that $\mathbf1_ F\mu _ u=\mathbf1_ F\mu _ f$. Take smooth functions $\phi _ j\downarrow u$ as in lemma 16.2.30, and set

\[ \psi _ j=P_\kappa (\min \{ \phi _ j,f\} ). \]

Each $\psi _ j$ has bounded metric Laplacian by theorem 16.2.24. Since $u\leq \phi _ j$ and $u\leq f$, the function $u$ is a competitor for $\min \{ \phi _ j,f\} $, and since this obstacle is continuous, proposition 16.1.4(1),(2) give $u\leq \psi _ j\leq f$. The obstacles $\min \{ \phi _ j,f\} $ decrease to $\min \{ u,f\} =u$, so $\psi _ j$ decreases to $P_\kappa (u)$ by proposition 16.1.4(5), and $P_\kappa (u)=u$, because $u$ is itself a competitor and every competitor lies below the upper semicontinuous obstacle $u$ by proposition 16.1.4(2). Moreover $\psi _ j=f$ on $F$, because $u\leq \psi _ j\leq f$. The regular contact identity, proposition 16.2.9, applied on the contact set $\{ \psi _ j=f\} \supset F$, therefore gives

\[ \mu _{\psi _ j}\geq \nu , \qquad \nu =\mathbf1_ F\mu _ f. \]

To pass to the limit, choose $C>0$ with $\min _ Xf>-C$, fix $\varepsilon >0$, and put

\[ \begin{aligned} \psi _ j^ C& =\max \{ \psi _ j,-C\} ,& u^ C& =\max \{ u,-C\} ,\\ b_ j& =\frac{\psi _ j^ C+C}{\psi _ j^ C+C+\varepsilon },& b& =\frac{u^ C+C}{u^ C+C+\varepsilon }. \end{aligned} \]

The bounded $\kappa $-psh functions $\psi _ j^ C$ decrease to $u^ C$, so they converge locally in capacity by proposition 9.4.6. The weights $b_ j$ and $b$ are bounded by one, and they are quasi-continuous, since $\psi _ j^ C$ and $u^ C$ are, by theorem 9.4.2 applied to their sums with smooth local potentials of $\kappa $; moreover $b_ j\to b$ in capacity, because the map $t\mapsto t/(t+\varepsilon )$ on $[0,\infty )$ has Lipschitz constant at most $1/\varepsilon $. The weighted form of theorem 9.4.8, applied with smooth local potentials of $\kappa $ and a finite partition of unity, gives

\[ b_ j\mu _{\psi _ j^ C}\longrightarrow b\mu _{u^ C} \quad \text{weakly on }X. \]

The measures $\mu _{\psi _ j^ C}$ and $\mu _{\psi _ j}$ agree on the plurifine open set $\{ \psi _ j>-C\} $, by plurifine locality (proposition 15.1.5), and $b_ j$ vanishes on $\{ \psi _ j\leq -C\} $; hence $b_ j\mu _{\psi _ j^ C}=b_ j\mu _{\psi _ j}$. In the same way $b\mu _{u^ C}=b\mu _ u$. On $F$ all the weights take the same continuous value $b_ f=(f+C)/(f+C+\varepsilon )$, which is strictly positive on $X$. Since $\nu $ is carried by $F$, we get $b_ j\mu _{\psi _ j}\geq b_ j\nu =b_ f\nu $ for every $j$, and in the weak limit $b\mu _ u\geq b_ f\nu $. Restricting to $F$, where $b=b_ f$, and dividing by $b_ f$, whose reciprocal is bounded on $X$, we obtain $\mathbf1_ F\mu _ u\geq \nu $. The reverse inequality is the ordered contact inequality of lemma 16.2.2. This proves the scalar identity for a Kähler background and a plurisubharmonic obstacle.

Step 2: obstacles that are psh for a larger background. Let $\theta $ be any closed smooth real $(1,1)$-form, let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and suppose that $u\leq f$ and that $f$ is $(\theta +A\omega )$-psh for some $A>0$. Increasing $A$, we may assume that $\theta +A\omega $ is Kähler. For every $t>A$, Step 1 applies to the Kähler background $\theta +t\omega $, with the same measuring metric $\omega $. For a continuous real function $g$ on $X$ consider

\[ Q_ g(t)=\int _{\{ u=f\} }g\, \langle (\theta +t\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\rangle -\int _{\{ u=f\} }g\, (\theta +t\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f)^ n. \]

This is a polynomial in $t$. Indeed, the first integrand expands by the multilinearity of the non-pluripolar product and its compatibility with smooth bounded factors (proposition 15.1.5, proposition 15.3.3), and the second by expanding the wedge products of the weak forms. Every coefficient is a finite measure, by theorem 15.4.3 and corollary 16.2.12. Step 1 gives $Q_ g(t)=0$ for all $t>A$, so every coefficient vanishes, and in particular $Q_ g(0)=0$. Now a finite signed Borel measure $\sigma $ on $X$ with $\int g\, \mathrm d\sigma =0$ for every continuous $g$ vanishes. Indeed, write $\sigma =\sigma ^+-\sigma ^-$ with finite positive Borel measures $\sigma ^\pm $; then $\int g\, \mathrm d\sigma ^+=\int g\, \mathrm d\sigma ^-$ for every continuous $g\geq 0$, so $\sigma ^+\leq \sigma ^-$ and $\sigma ^-\leq \sigma ^+$ on Borel sets by lemma 16.2.1(2), applied with $W=X$. Applied to the difference of the two measures in $Q_ g(0)$, this proves the scalar identity whenever $f$ is $(\theta +A\omega )$-psh for some $A$.

Step 3: general obstacles. Let $f$ be any continuous real function with bounded metric Laplacian, and $u\leq f$. Choose $A>0$ such that $\kappa =\theta +A\omega $ is Kähler, and insert the envelope $v=P_\kappa (f)$:

\[ u\leq v\leq f. \]

Here $u\leq v$ because $u$ is $\kappa $-psh and lies below $f$. By theorem 16.2.24, $v$ is a continuous $\kappa $-psh function with bounded metric Laplacian. Step 2, with $v$ as the obstacle, gives

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ u=v\} }\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\rangle =\mathbf1_{\{ u=v\} }(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n. \]

Both $v$ and $f$ belong to $W^{2,p}$ for every finite $p$, by proposition 16.2.11, and the locality of weak Hessians (proposition 9.12.3) shows that their weak second derivatives agree almost everywhere on $\{ v=f\} $. Since $\{ u=f\} =\{ u=v\} \cap \{ v=f\} $, restricting the last identity to $\{ u=f\} $ proves the scalar identity for $f$.

Step 4: the mixed identity. For $\lambda _1,\ldots ,\lambda _ n>0$ put $\theta _\lambda =\sum _ j\lambda _ j\theta _ j$, $u_\lambda =\sum _ j\lambda _ ju_ j$ and $f_\lambda =\sum _ j\lambda _ jf_ j$. Then $u_\lambda $ is $\theta _\lambda $-psh, $f_\lambda $ is continuous with bounded metric Laplacian, $u_\lambda \leq f_\lambda $, and $E\subset \{ u_\lambda =f_\lambda \} $. (Since the $f_ j$ are finite, $E$ contains no pole of any $u_ j$.) We restrict the scalar identity for $u_\lambda $ and $f_\lambda $ to $E$ and expand both sides as measure-valued polynomials in $\lambda $, using, as in Step 2, the multilinearity of the non-pluripolar product (proposition 15.1.5) and of the wedge product of the weak forms. Testing against a continuous function and comparing the coefficients of $\lambda _1\cdots \lambda _ n$, which are $n!$ times the integrals of this function against the two sides of (16.66), we see that these integrals agree; by the uniqueness proved in Step 2, we obtain (16.66).

Step 5: the density on $E$. For each $j$ choose $A_ j>0$ such that $\theta _ j+A_ j\omega $ is Kähler, and insert the envelope $v_ j=P_{\theta _ j+A_ j\omega }(f_ j)$. Since $u_ j$ is $(\theta _ j+A_ j\omega )$-psh and lies below the continuous function $f_ j$, we have $u_ j\leq v_ j\leq f_ j$ by proposition 16.1.4(1),(2), so $v_ j=f_ j$ on $\{ u_ j=f_ j\} $; as in Step 3, proposition 16.2.11, proposition 9.12.3 show that the weak Hessians of $v_ j$ and $f_ j$ agree almost everywhere there. The positive form $\theta _ j+A_ j\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j$ has bounded coefficients by theorem 16.2.24, so $\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j$ has bounded coefficients almost everywhere on $\{ u_ j=f_ j\} $. Hence the density of the mixed product of the weak forms is bounded on $E$. It is nonnegative there, because by (16.66) its restriction to $E$ equals the restriction of a positive non-pluripolar product. $\square$

Corollary 16.2.32. Suppose that $\{ \theta \} $ is big, and let $f$ be a continuous real function with bounded metric Laplacian. Then

\[ \langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P_\theta (f))^ n\rangle =\mathbf1_{\{ P_\theta (f)=f\} }(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f)^ n. \]

In particular $\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_\theta )^ n\rangle =\mathbf1_{\{ V_\theta =0\} }\theta ^ n$. The same identities hold in every pseudo-effective class; this is corollary 17.4.22 in the next chapter, which adds a zero-mass argument for classes that are not big.

Proof. A constant shift of $V_\theta $ lies below the bounded function $f$, so the competitor family of $P_\theta (f)$ is nonempty, and it is bounded above by $\max _ Xf$. Since $f$ is continuous, $u=P_\theta (f)$ satisfies $u\leq f$ everywhere, by proposition 16.1.4. Theorem 16.2.31, with all $\theta _ j=\theta $, $u_ j=u$ and $f_ j=f$, identifies the restriction of $\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\rangle $ to $\{ u=f\} $ with that of $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f)^ n$. By theorem 16.2.5, applied to the continuous and hence quasi-continuous obstacle $f$, the measure $\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\rangle $ is carried by $\{ u=f\} $. This proves the first identity. For $f=0$ the envelope is $V_\theta $ by definition, which gives the second. $\square$

16.3 Singularity envelopes

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

We now replace a pointwise obstacle by a constraint on singularities: we take the upper envelope of all nonpositive potentials that are more singular than a given potential $u$. Since the constant $C$ in $v\leq u+C$ may depend on the competitor $v$, the envelope can be less singular than $u$; this happens in example 16.4.8 below.

Definition 16.3.1. For $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, the singularity envelope of $u$ is

\[ P_\theta [u]=\bigl (\sup \{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):v\leq 0, \ v\preceq u\} \bigr )^*. \]

The functions $v$ in this supremum are the competitors for $P_\theta [u]$. A model potential is a potential $\phi $ with $P_\theta [\phi ]=\phi $ and positive mass $\int _ X\langle \theta _\phi ^ n\rangle >0$. Since $u-\sup _ Xu$ is a competitor and all competitors are nonpositive, the singularity envelope is always defined, belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and satisfies $u-\sup _ Xu\leq P_\theta [u]\leq V_\theta $ and $\sup _ XP_\theta [u]=0$.

Proposition 16.3.2. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

  1. $P_\theta [u]$ depends only on the singularity type of $u$, and $u\preceq u'$ implies $P_\theta [u]\leq P_\theta [u']$.

  2. The rooftop envelopes $P_\theta (u+k,V_\theta )$ increase with $k$, and

    \[ P_\theta [u]=\left(\lim _{k\to \infty } P_\theta (u+k,V_\theta )\right)^*. \]

  3. The singularity envelope preserves all mixed non-pluripolar masses: if $\theta _1,\ldots ,\theta _ p$, $1\leq p\leq n$, are smooth closed real $(1,1)$-forms and $u_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$, then

    \[ \int _ X\left\langle \bigwedge _ j(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P_{\theta _ j}[u_ j])\right\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p} =\int _ X\left\langle \bigwedge _ j(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)\right\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p}. \]

    In particular, $P_\theta [u]$ has the same mass as $u$.

  4. If $u$ has minimal singularities, then $P_\theta [u]=V_\theta $.

Proof. (1) The family of competitors depends only on the singularity type of $u$, and it increases when $u$ is replaced by a less singular potential.

(2) The rooftop envelopes increase with $k$ because the rooftop envelope is monotone. Every competitor $v$ for $P_\theta [u]$ satisfies $v\leq u+k$ for some integer $k$ and $v\leq V_\theta $, hence $v\leq P_\theta (u+k,V_\theta )$. Conversely, each $P_\theta (u+k,V_\theta )$ is nonpositive and, by proposition 16.1.6, has the singularity type of $u$; so it is a competitor. Hence the two families have the same supremum, which proves the formula.

(3) By proposition 16.1.6, the tuple of rooftop envelopes $P_{\theta _ j}(u_ j+k,V_{\theta _ j})$ has the singularity types of the $u_ j$, so it has the same mixed mass as the tuple $(u_ j)$ by corollary 15.7.5. By (2) and theorem 8.3.2, which shows that an increasing sequence differs from its regularized limit only on a Lebesgue null set, these rooftop envelopes increase almost everywhere to $P_{\theta _ j}[u_ j]$ as $k\to \infty $, and corollary 15.7.6 shows that their mixed masses converge to the mixed mass of the envelopes.

(4) If $u$ has minimal singularities, then $V_\theta \preceq u$, so $V_\theta $ is itself a competitor; it dominates all other competitors, since they are nonpositive. $\square$

Proposition 16.3.3. Suppose that $\{ \theta \} $ is big, let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and put $w=P_\theta [u]$. Then

\[ \mu _ w\leq \mathbf1_{\{ w=V_\theta \} }\mu _{V_\theta }, \]

and $\mu _ w$ is carried by $\{ w=0\} $.

Proof. Put $w_ k=P_\theta (u+k,V_\theta )$ for integers $k$; these are potentials by proposition 16.1.6. By corollary 16.2.6,

\[ \mu _{w_ k}\leq \mathbf1_{\{ w_ k=u+k\} }\mu _ u+\mathbf1_{\{ w_ k=V_\theta \} }\mu _{V_\theta } \leq \mathbf1_{\{ u+k\leq V_\theta \} }\mu _ u +\mathbf1_{\{ w=V_\theta \} }\mu _{V_\theta }; \]

for the second inequality we used $w_ k\leq V_\theta $ and $w_ k\leq w\leq V_\theta $. Since $V_\theta \leq 0$, the sets $\{ u+k\leq V_\theta \} $ decrease to the pole set of $u$, which is $\mu _ u$-null, and the first term tends to zero in total variation by dominated convergence. By proposition 16.3.2, $w_ k$ increases almost everywhere to $w$, so $\mu _{w_ k}\to \mu _ w$ weakly by corollary 15.7.6. Integrating a continuous function $\chi \geq 0$ and letting $k\to \infty $, we obtain $\int _ X\chi \, \mu _ w\leq \int _ X\chi \mathbf1_{\{ w=V_\theta \} }\, \mu _{V_\theta }$, and lemma 16.2.1 gives the asserted inequality. Finally, a potential lies below $0$ quasi-everywhere if and only if it is nonpositive, so $V_\theta =P_\theta (0)$, and theorem 16.2.5 shows that $\mu _{V_\theta }$ is carried by $\{ V_\theta =0\} $. Hence $\mu _ w$ is carried by $\{ w=V_\theta =0\} $. $\square$

16.4 Extrapolation and model singularities

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

Throughout this section $\{ \theta \} $ is big, and all potentials belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Mass is monotone with respect to $\preceq $, but two potentials with the same mass need not have bounded difference (example 16.4.8). The basic step reverses a convex combination: if $u\preceq v$ and the mass lost from $v$ to $u$ is small, then $u\geq a^{-1}\eta +(1-a^{-1})v$ for some $a>1$ and some potential $\eta $ of positive mass. The contact concentration of section 16.2.1 prevents the envelopes used to construct $\eta $ from collapsing. This extrapolation yields the domination principle and identifies the singularity envelopes of potentials of positive mass as model potentials.

Lemma 16.4.1. Let $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\psi \leq 0$ and $\int _ X\langle \theta _\psi ^ n\rangle >0$. Then there is a model potential $\phi \geq \psi $ with $\int _ X\langle \theta _\phi ^ n\rangle =\int _ X\langle \theta _\psi ^ n\rangle $.

Proof. Fix a smooth probability volume form $\mathrm dV$ on $X$, put $m=\int _ X\langle \theta _\psi ^ n\rangle $, and consider

\[ \mathcal S=\{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):\psi \leq v\leq 0, \ \int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle =m\} , \qquad H(v)=\int _ Xv\, \mathrm dV. \]

The set $\mathcal S$ contains $\psi $, and $H$ takes values in $[H(\psi ),0]$ on it. We construct an increasing sequence in $\mathcal S$ that almost maximizes $H$ at every stage. Put $v_0=\psi $. Given $v_ k\in \mathcal S$, let

\[ s_ k=\sup \{ H(w):w\in \mathcal S,\ w\geq v_ k\} , \]

and choose $v_{k+1}\in \mathcal S$ with $v_{k+1}\geq v_ k$ and $H(v_{k+1})\geq s_ k-2^{-k}$. The regularized limit $\phi =(\sup _ kv_ k)^*$ is a potential with $\psi \leq \phi \leq 0$, and $\int _ X\langle \theta _\phi ^ n\rangle =m$ by corollary 15.7.6; thus $\phi \in \mathcal S$. Since $v_ k$ increases to $\phi $ almost everywhere, between the integrable bounds $\psi $ and $0$, dominated convergence gives $H(v_ k)\to H(\phi )$.

Let $w\in \mathcal S$ with $w\geq \phi $. Then $w\geq v_ k$ for every $k$, so

\[ H(w)\leq s_ k\leq H(v_{k+1})+2^{-k}, \]

and letting $k\to \infty $ gives $H(w)\leq H(\phi )$. Since $w\geq \phi $, it follows that $w=\phi $ almost everywhere, hence everywhere. Now $P_\theta [\phi ]$ lies between $\phi $ and $0$, because $\phi \leq 0$ is a competitor for it, and it has mass $m$ by proposition 16.3.2. Thus $P_\theta [\phi ]\in \mathcal S$ and $P_\theta [\phi ]\geq \phi $, so $P_\theta [\phi ]=\phi $. Since $m>0$, $\phi $ is a model potential. $\square$

Lemma 16.4.2. Let $\phi ,\tau \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $P_\theta [\phi ]=\phi $ and $\tau \preceq \phi $. Then

\[ \sup _{\{ \phi > -\infty \} }(\tau -\phi )=\sup _ X\tau . \]

In particular, if $\tau \leq 0$, then $\tau \leq \phi $.

Proof. The potential $\tau -\sup _ X\tau $ is nonpositive and more singular than $\phi $, so it is a competitor for $P_\theta [\phi ]=\phi $. Hence $\tau -\phi \leq \sup _ X\tau $ wherever $\phi $ is finite. Conversely, $\phi \leq 0$, so $\tau -\phi \geq \tau $ wherever $\phi $ is finite. The pole set of $\phi $ is pluripolar, and removing a pluripolar set does not change the supremum of the quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\tau $: an upper bound for $\tau $ off a Lebesgue null set holds everywhere. This proves the reverse inequality. $\square$

The envelopes used for extrapolation have obstacles of the form $au+(1-a)v$ with $a>1$. The following lemma collects their basic properties.

Lemma 16.4.3. Let $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\leq v$, let $a>1$, and put $f=au+(1-a)v$, where $f=-\infty $ on the pole set of $u$, which contains that of $v$.

  1. The obstacle $f$ is quasi-continuous, and $f\leq u$. A potential $\eta $ satisfies

    \[ a^{-1}\eta +(1-a^{-1})v\leq u \]

    if and only if $\eta \leq f$ quasi-everywhere.

  2. Suppose that $f$ has a competitor. Then $\eta =P_\theta (f)$ is the largest potential satisfying this inequality, and $\eta \leq u$. Moreover, if $\gamma =a^{-1}\eta +(1-a^{-1})v$ and $D=\{ \gamma =u>-\infty \} $, then

    \[ \mu _\eta \leq a^ n\mathbf1_ D\mu _ u. \]

Proof. (1) Off the pole set of $u$, the functions $u$ and $v$ are finite and $f=u+(a-1)(u-v)\leq u$, because $u\leq v$; on the pole set, $f=-\infty $. Thus $f$ is a Borel function with $f\leq u$. Let $K$ be a compact subset of a chart. Since $u$ and $v$ are quasi-continuous (lemma 16.1.2(1)), there are open sets of arbitrarily small capacity outside which both are continuous on $K$, as functions with values in $[-\infty ,\infty )$. On the complement in $K$ of such a set, $f$ is continuous at every point where $u$ is finite, because $v\geq u$ is finite near such a point, and at a pole of $u$ it is continuous with value $-\infty $, because $f\leq u$. Hence $f$ is quasi-continuous. Off the pole set of $u$, the inequalities $\eta \leq f$ and $a^{-1}\eta +(1-a^{-1})v\leq u$ are equivalent. Since the pole set is pluripolar, and an inequality between quasi-plurisubharmonic functions that holds quasi-everywhere holds everywhere, this proves the equivalence.

(2) By (1), the potentials satisfying the inequality are exactly the competitors for $f$; since $f\leq u$, they lie below $u$ quasi-everywhere, hence everywhere. Thus $\mathcal A_\theta (f)$ is nonempty, by assumption, and uniformly bounded above, and proposition 16.1.4 shows that $\eta $ is its largest member; in particular $\eta \leq u$ and $\gamma \leq u$. By theorem 16.2.5, $\mu _\eta $ is carried by $\{ \eta =f\} $. At a point of this set at which $u$ is finite, $\eta =au+(1-a)v$ means $\gamma =u$; since $\mu _\eta $ does not charge the pole set of $u$, it is carried by $D$. Moreover $\mu _\gamma \geq a^{-n}\mu _\eta $, and lemma 16.2.2, applied to $\gamma \leq u$, gives $\mathbf1_ D\mu _\gamma \leq \mathbf1_ D\mu _ u$. Hence

\[ \mu _\eta =\mathbf1_ D\mu _\eta \leq a^ n\mathbf1_ D\mu _\gamma \leq a^ n\mathbf1_ D\mu _ u. \]
$\square$

Theorem 16.4.4. Let $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\preceq v$ and $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle >0$, and let $a>1$ satisfy

16.69
\begin{equation} a^ n\left(\int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle -\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle \right)<\int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle . \end{equation}

Then there exists $\eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\int _ X\langle \theta _\eta ^ n\rangle >0$ such that

16.70
\begin{equation} a^{-1}\eta +(1-a^{-1})v\leq u. \end{equation}

The largest such $\eta $ is the obstacle envelope $E_ a(u,v)=P_\theta (au+(1-a)v)$; here the obstacle $au+(1-a)v$ is defined to be $-\infty $ on the pole set of $u$, which contains that of $v$. If $u$ and $v$ have the same mass, then (16.69) holds for every $a>1$, and $E_ a(u,v)$ also has this mass.

Proof. We first construct one potential satisfying (16.70) as the limit of a decreasing sequence of envelopes, and then show that $E_ a(u,v)$ is the largest solution and has positive mass.

Reduction. Since $u\preceq v$, there are constants $A,B$ with $u-A\leq v-B\leq 0$. If $\eta '$ satisfies (16.70) for the pair $u-A$, $v-B$, then $\eta '+aA+(1-a)B$ satisfies it for $u$, $v$; the envelopes of the two pairs differ by the same constant, and masses do not change. We may therefore assume that $u\leq v\leq 0$. Then $m=\int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle \geq \int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle >0$, and lemma 16.4.1 provides a model potential $\phi \geq v$ with $\int _ X\langle \theta _\phi ^ n\rangle =m$. Since $1-a<0$, we have $au+(1-a)\phi \leq au+(1-a)v$; we first construct a potential below $au+(1-a)\phi $.

A decreasing sequence of envelopes. For $j\geq 1$ put $u_ j=\max \{ u,\phi -j\} $, so that $\phi -j\leq u_ j\leq \phi $; in particular, $u_ j$ and $\phi $ have the same pole set. Put

\[ f_ j=au_ j+(1-a)\phi ,\qquad \eta _ j=P_\theta (f_ j), \]

where $f_ j=-\infty $ on the pole set of $\phi $, as in lemma 16.4.3 for the pair $u_ j\leq \phi $. Outside this pole set, $f_ j=\phi +a(u_ j-\phi )$ lies between $\phi -aj$ and $\phi $. Hence the potential $\phi -aj$ is a competitor, and every competitor is at most $\phi $, so $\eta _ j$ is a potential with

\[ \phi -aj\leq \eta _ j\leq \phi ,\qquad \int _ X\langle \theta _{\eta _ j}^ n\rangle =\int _ X\langle \theta _{u_ j}^ n\rangle =m, \]

where the masses agree because $\eta _ j\sim \phi \sim u_ j$. Since $u_ j$ decreases with $j$, so do $f_ j$ and $\eta _ j$.

A contact bound. Put $\gamma _ j=a^{-1}\eta _ j+(1-a^{-1})\phi $ and $D_ j=\{ \gamma _ j=u_ j>-\infty \} $. By lemma 16.4.3(2), applied to the pair $u_ j\leq \phi $, we have $\gamma _ j\leq u_ j$ and

16.71
\begin{equation} \mu _{\eta _ j}\leq a^ n\mathbf1_{D_ j}\mu _{u_ j}. \end{equation}

A tail estimate. Fix $k>0$ and let $j>k$. On $D_ j$ we have $\eta _ j=\phi +a(u_ j-\phi )$, so there $\eta _ j\leq \phi -ak$ if and only if $u_ j\leq \phi -k$, that is, if and only if $u\leq \phi -k$. The plurifine open set $\{ u>\phi -k\} $ is contained in $\{ u>\phi -j\} $, on which $u_ j=u$; hence $\mu _{u_ j}=\mu _ u$ on $\{ u>\phi -k\} $, and $\mu _{u_ j}(\{ u\leq \phi -k\} )=m-\mu _ u(\{ u>\phi -k\} )$, pole sets having measure zero. Together with (16.71) this gives

16.72
\begin{equation} \mu _{\eta _ j}(\{ \eta _ j\leq \phi -ak\} ) \leq a^ n\left(m-\mu _ u(\{ u>\phi -k\} )\right). \end{equation}

The envelopes do not collapse. Suppose that $\eta _ j$ decreases to $-\infty $ identically. Then $\sup _ X\eta _ j\to -\infty $ by corollary 14.2.5. Since $\eta _ j\preceq \phi $ and $\phi $ is a model potential, lemma 16.4.2 gives $\eta _ j-\phi \leq \sup _ X\eta _ j$ off the pole set of $\phi $. Hence, for each $k$, $\eta _ j\leq \phi -ak$ everywhere once $j$ is large, and the left side of (16.72) equals $\int _ X\langle \theta _{\eta _ j}^ n\rangle =m$. Since $\phi $ is finite wherever $u$ is, $\mu _ u(\{ u>\phi -k\} )\to \int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle $ as $k\to \infty $, and (16.72) yields $m\leq a^ n\bigl (m-\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle \bigr )$, which contradicts (16.69). Consequently $\eta _ j$ decreases to a potential $\eta $. Letting $j\to \infty $ in $\gamma _ j\leq u_ j$ gives $a^{-1}\eta +(1-a^{-1})\phi \leq u$, and since $v\leq \phi $, also (16.70).

The largest solution. By lemma 16.4.3(1), applied to the pair $u\leq v$, the potentials satisfying (16.70) are exactly the competitors for the obstacle $f=au+(1-a)v$. The potential $\eta $ constructed above is one of them, so part (2) of the lemma shows that $E_ a=E_ a(u,v)=P_\theta (f)$ is the largest potential satisfying (16.70), and that $E_ a\leq u$.

Positive mass. Choose $b>a$ that still satisfies (16.69). The preceding steps, applied to $b$, give $E_ b=E_ b(u,v)$. The potential

\[ q=\frac abE_ b+\left(1-\frac ab\right)u \]

is a competitor for the obstacle $f$ of $E_ a$: quasi-everywhere, $E_ b\leq bu+(1-b)v$, and therefore

\[ q\leq au+(1-a)v+\left(1-\frac ab\right)(u-v)\leq au+(1-a)v. \]

Hence $q\leq E_ a$, and the monotonicity of mass together with $\mu _ q\geq (1-a/b)^ n\mu _ u$ yields

\[ \int _ X\langle \theta _{E_ a}^ n\rangle \geq \int _ X\langle \theta _ q^ n\rangle \geq \left(1-\frac ab\right)^ n\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle >0. \]

If $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle =\int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle $, then (16.69) holds for every $a>1$, so $b$ can be taken arbitrarily large, and we obtain $\int _ X\langle \theta _{E_ a}^ n\rangle \geq \int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle $. The reverse inequality follows from $E_ a\leq u$. $\square$

Lemma 16.4.5. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle >0$. Then there are $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $v\leq u$ and $\delta >0$ such that $\theta _ v\geq \delta \omega $.

Proof. Put $m=\int _ X\langle \theta _{V_\theta }^ n\rangle $. Since $u\preceq V_\theta $ and $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle >0$, we can choose $a>1$ so close to one that $a^ n\bigl (m-\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle \bigr )<m$. Theorem 16.4.4 gives $\eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $a^{-1}\eta +(1-a^{-1})V_\theta \leq u$. Since the class is big, it contains a closed current $T\geq \delta _0\omega $ for some $\delta _0>0$, and by theorem 14.1.7 we can write $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi $ with $\psi $ quasi-plurisubharmonic; after subtracting a constant, $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies $\psi \leq 0$ and $\theta _\psi \geq \delta _0\omega $, and $\psi \leq V_\theta $ because $\psi $ is a competitor for $V_\theta $. Therefore

\[ v=a^{-1}\eta +(1-a^{-1})\psi \leq u,\qquad \theta _ v=a^{-1}\theta _\eta +(1-a^{-1})\theta _\psi \geq (1-a^{-1})\delta _0\omega . \]
$\square$

Recall from definition 15.8.3 that, for $\chi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, the class $\mathcal E(X,\theta ,\chi )$ of potentials of full mass relative to $\chi $ consists of the potentials $u\preceq \chi $ with $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle =\int _ X\langle \theta _\chi ^ n\rangle $.

Theorem 16.4.6 (Domination principle). Let $\chi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\int _ X\langle \theta _\chi ^ n\rangle >0$, and let $u,v\in \mathcal E(X,\theta ,\chi )$. If $\mu _ u(\{ u<v\} )=0$, then $u\geq v$ everywhere on $X$.

Proof. After reducing to the case $u\leq v$, we show that the extrapolation envelopes $E_ a(u,v)$ agree with $u$ almost everywhere for $\mu _ u$. Hence they do not collapse as $a\to \infty $, while their limit equals $-\infty $ quasi-everywhere on $\{ u<v\} $, so that this set is pluripolar.

The potential $\max \{ u,v\} $ is more singular than $\chi $ and less singular than $u$, so

\[ \int _ X\langle \theta _\chi ^ n\rangle \geq \int _ X\langle \theta _{\max \{ u,v\} }^ n\rangle \geq \int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle =\int _ X\langle \theta _\chi ^ n\rangle ; \]

thus $\max \{ u,v\} \in \mathcal E(X,\theta ,\chi )$. Since $\{ u<\max \{ u,v\} \} =\{ u<v\} $, we may replace $v$ by $\max \{ u,v\} $ and assume that $u\leq v$. Subtracting the same constant from $u$ and $v$, we may also assume $v\leq 0$. Let $m>0$ be the common mass of $u$, $v$ and $\chi $.

For $a>1$ let $\eta _ a=E_ a(u,v)$, $\gamma _ a=a^{-1}\eta _ a+(1-a^{-1})v$ and $D_ a=\{ \gamma _ a=u>-\infty \} $. By theorem 16.4.4, $\eta _ a$ is a potential of mass $m$; in particular its obstacle $au+(1-a)v$ has a competitor, and lemma 16.4.3(2), applied to the pair $u\leq v$, gives $\eta _ a\leq u$, $\gamma _ a\leq u$ and

\[ \mu _{\eta _ a}\leq a^ n\mathbf1_{D_ a}\mu _ u. \]

Since $u\leq v$, the hypothesis means that $\mu _ u$ is carried by $\{ u=v\} $, so $\mu _{\eta _ a}$ is carried by $D_ a\cap \{ u=v\} $. At a point of this set, $a^{-1}\eta _ a+(1-a^{-1})u=u$, that is, $\eta _ a=u$. Hence $\mu _{\eta _ a}$ is carried by $\{ \eta _ a=u\} $, and lemma 16.2.2, applied to $\eta _ a\leq u$, gives $\mu _{\eta _ a}\leq \mathbf1_{\{ \eta _ a=u\} }\mu _ u\leq \mu _ u$. Both measures have mass $m$, so they are equal, and $\eta _ a=u$ holds $\mu _ u$-almost everywhere. In particular

\[ \int _ X\mathrm e^{\eta _ a}\, \mu _ u =\int _ X\mathrm e^ u\, \mu _ u>0, \]

where the last integral is positive because $m>0$ and $\mu _ u$ does not charge the pole set of $u$.

As $a$ increases, the obstacle $v+a(u-v)$ decreases, so $\eta _ a$ decreases. If $\eta _ a$ decreased to $-\infty $ identically as $a\to \infty $ through the integers, dominated convergence, with $0\leq \mathrm e^{\eta _ a}\leq 1$, would make the left side of the last display tend to zero, a contradiction. Hence the limit $\eta $ along the integers is a potential. Each $\eta _ a$ satisfies $\eta _ a\leq v+a(u-v)$ outside a pluripolar set; let $N$ be the union of these sets over the integers $a\geq 2$. At a point outside $N$ where $u$ is finite and $u<v$, we get $\eta \leq v+a(u-v)$ for every integer $a$, hence $\eta =-\infty $. Thus $\{ u<v\} $ is contained in the union of $N$ and the pole sets of $u$ and $\eta $, which is pluripolar. Therefore $u\geq v$ quasi-everywhere, and hence everywhere. $\square$

Theorem 16.4.7. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle >0$. Then

\[ P_\theta [u]=\max \{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):v\leq 0, \ u\preceq v,\ \int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle =\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle \} . \]

In particular $P_\theta [P_\theta [u]]=P_\theta [u]$, so $P_\theta [u]$ is a model potential of mass $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle $.

Proof. Write $w=P_\theta [u]$, and let $v$ belong to the displayed family. By proposition 16.3.2, $w\leq P_\theta [v]$, and the potentials $w$, $u$, $v$ and $P_\theta [v]$ all have the same positive mass. Moreover $v\leq P_\theta [v]$, because $v\leq 0$ is a competitor for $P_\theta [v]$. Hence $w$ and $v$ both belong to $\mathcal E(X,\theta ,P_\theta [v])$, a class whose reference potential has positive mass. By proposition 16.3.3, $\mu _ w$ is carried by $\{ w=0\} $, and since $v\leq 0$, it gives no mass to $\{ w<v\} $. The domination principle, theorem 16.4.6, yields $w\geq v$. Finally, $w$ itself belongs to the family: it is nonpositive, it satisfies $u-\sup _ Xu\leq w$, and it has the same mass as $u$ by proposition 16.3.2. This proves the formula.

The potential $P_\theta [w]$ is nonpositive, satisfies $u\preceq w\preceq P_\theta [w]$, and has the same mass as $u$ by proposition 16.3.2. Hence it belongs to the family, and the formula gives $P_\theta [w]\leq w$. Conversely, $w\leq 0$ is a competitor for $P_\theta [w]$, so $P_\theta [w]=w$. Since the mass of $w$ is positive, $w$ is a model potential. $\square$

Example 16.4.8. On $\mathbb P^1$ let $\omega =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (1+|z|^2)$ in the affine coordinate $z$, so that $\int _{\mathbb P^1}\omega =1$; since $\omega $ is Kähler, $V_\omega =0$. Define

\[ F(r)=\begin{cases} \max \{ -\log (-r),r+2\} ,& r\leq -1,\\ r+2,& r\geq -1, \end{cases} \qquad u(z)=F(\log |z|^2)-\log (1+|z|^2). \]

The function $F$ is continuous and nondecreasing. Since $-\log (-r)<r+2$ at $r=-1$, there is $\delta >0$ with $F(r)=r+2$ for $r\geq -1-\delta $, and since $r+2+\log (-r)\to -\infty $ as $r\to -\infty $, we have $F(r)=-\log (-r)$ for all sufficiently negative $r$.

We first show that $F(\log |z|^2)$ is subharmonic on $\mathbb C\setminus \{ 0\} $. On the open set $\{ |z|^2>\mathrm e^{-1-\delta }\} $ it equals the harmonic function $\log |z|^2+2$. On the open set $\{ 0<|z|^2<\mathrm e^{-1}\} $ it equals $\max \{ g(\log |z|^2),\log |z|^2+2\} $, where $g(t)=-\log (-t)$ for $t<0$. Since $g''(t)=t^{-2}>0$, the function $g$ is convex, so it is the supremum of its tangent lines $t\mapsto g(s)+g'(s)(t-s)$, $s<0$, whose slopes $g'(s)=-1/s$ are positive. Hence $g(\log |z|^2)$ is a continuous supremum of the harmonic functions $g(s)+g'(s)(\log |z|^2-s)$; it therefore satisfies the submean inequality and is subharmonic, and so is its maximum with $\log |z|^2+2$. Since subharmonicity is a local property, $F(\log |z|^2)$ is subharmonic on $\mathbb C\setminus \{ 0\} $; it tends to $-\infty $ at $0$ and extends subharmonically across the polar set $\{ 0\} $ by theorem 3.5.2.

Next we check that $u$ is $\omega $-plurisubharmonic. For $|z|^2\geq \mathrm e^{-1}$ we have $u=2-\log (1+|z|^{-2})$. In the coordinate $\zeta =1/z$ near $\infty $ this reads $u=2-\log (1+|\zeta |^2)$, while $\omega =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (1+|\zeta |^2)$, because $\log (1+|z|^2)=\log (1+|\zeta |^2)-\log |\zeta |^2$ and $\log |\zeta |^2$ is pluriharmonic for $\zeta \ne 0$; thus $u$ is smooth near $\infty $, with $u(\infty )=2$ and $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=0$ there. Hence $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^1,\omega )$, with $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F(\log |z|^2)$ on $\mathbb C$, and its only pole is $0$.

To compute the mass of $u$, note that its Lelong number at $0$ is that of the subharmonic function $F(\log |z|^2)$, which differs from $u$ by a smooth function, and since $F$ is continuous and nondecreasing, $\sup _{|z|<\mathrm e^{t/2}}F(\log |z|^2)=F(t)$. By proposition 12.1.1, the Lelong number is therefore

\[ \lim _{t\to -\infty }\frac{F(t)}t=\lim _{t\to -\infty }\frac{-\log (-t)}t=0. \]

By theorem 12.1.3, in one variable the mass that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F(\log |z|^2)$ gives to $0$ is this Lelong number, namely the limit of the masses of the discs $B(0,r)$ as $r\downarrow 0$, so the current $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ has no atom at its pole. By proposition 15.3.6, $\mu _ u$ is the restriction of $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ to $\{ u>-\infty \} $, hence the mass of $u$ is $\int _{\mathbb P^1}\langle \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\rangle =\int _{\mathbb P^1}\omega =1$.

The potential $0$ is nonpositive, less singular than $u$, and has the same mass $\int _{\mathbb P^1}\omega =1$, so theorem 16.4.7 gives $P_\omega [u]\geq 0$; since also $P_\omega [u]\leq V_\omega =0$, we obtain $P_\omega [u]=0$. Near $0$, however, $u=-\log (-\log |z|^2)+O(1)$ is unbounded below. Thus the singularity envelope can change the singularity type of a potential while preserving its full mass.

Definition 16.4.9. Let $u,\phi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\preceq \phi $. The singularity envelope of $u$ relative to $\phi $ is

\[ P_\theta [u](\phi ) =\left(\lim _{C\to \infty }P_\theta (u+C,\phi )\right)^*. \]

Choose $C_0$ with $u-C_0\leq \phi $. For $C\geq -C_0$, the potential $u-C_0$ is a common minorant of $u+C$ and $\phi $, so the rooftop envelope $P_\theta (u+C,\phi )$ lies between $u-C_0$ and $u+C$ and has the singularity type of $u$. These rooftop envelopes increase with $C$ and are bounded above by $\phi $, so the regularized limit is a potential between $u-C_0$ and $\phi $.

Proposition 16.4.10. Let $u,\phi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\preceq \phi $ and $\int _ X\langle \theta _\phi ^ n\rangle >0$. Then the following are equivalent:

  1. $u\in \mathcal E(X,\theta ,\phi )$;

  2. $P_\theta [u](\phi )=\phi $;

  3. $P_\theta [u]=P_\theta [\phi ]$.

Furthermore, the relative envelope $w=P_\theta [u](\phi )$ has the same mass as $u$, and

\[ \mu _ w\leq \mathbf1_{\{ w=\phi \} }\mu _\phi . \]

Proof. Write $w_ C=P_\theta (u+C,\phi )$. Each $w_ C$ with $C$ large has the singularity type of $u$, hence mass $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle $, and $w_ C$ increases almost everywhere to $w$; by corollary 15.7.6, $\int _ X\langle \theta _ w^ n\rangle =\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle $. By corollary 16.2.6, together with $w_ C\leq \phi $ and $w_ C\leq w\leq \phi $,

\[ \mu _{w_ C}\leq \mathbf1_{\{ u+C\leq \phi \} }\mu _ u +\mathbf1_{\{ w=\phi \} }\mu _\phi . \]

As $C\to \infty $, the sets $\{ u+C\leq \phi \} $ decrease to the pole set of $u$, which is $\mu _ u$-null, because $\phi $ is bounded above; so the first term tends to zero in total variation by dominated convergence. Moreover $\mu _{w_ C}\to \mu _ w$ weakly by corollary 15.7.6. Integrating continuous functions $\chi \geq 0$ and applying lemma 16.2.1, we obtain the asserted measure bound.

If (1) holds, then $w$ and $\phi $ have the same positive mass and both belong to $\mathcal E(X,\theta ,\phi )$. By the measure bound, $\mu _ w$ gives no mass to $\{ w<\phi \} $, and the domination principle, theorem 16.4.6, gives $w\geq \phi $; hence (2). Conversely, if (2) holds, then $u$ has the mass of $w=\phi $, which is (1).

To prove that (2) implies (3), normalize $\phi \leq 0$. This is harmless: replacing $\phi $ by $\phi +c$ replaces $w_ C$ by $P_\theta (u+C-c,\phi )+c$, hence $w$ by $w+c$, so (2) is preserved, and $P_\theta [u]$ and $P_\theta [\phi ]$ do not change. Every $w_ C$ is then a nonpositive potential with the singularity type of $u$, hence a competitor for $P_\theta [u]$, so $\phi =w\leq P_\theta [u]$. Since (2) implies (1), $u$ has the positive mass of $\phi $, so $P_\theta [u]$ is a model potential by theorem 16.4.7. Monotonicity and $u\preceq \phi $ now give

\[ P_\theta [\phi ]\leq P_\theta [P_\theta [u]]=P_\theta [u] \leq P_\theta [\phi ]. \]

Finally, if (3) holds, then by proposition 16.3.2 the potential $u$ has the mass of $P_\theta [u]=P_\theta [\phi ]$, which is the mass of $\phi $; this is (1). $\square$

16.5 Rooftops and mass inequalities

From Foundations of pluripotential theory

Two potentials need not have a common minorant, as example 16.1.7 shows. We prove that a strict mass inequality guarantees one, and that the masses of $u$, $v$, $\max \{ u,v\} $ and $P_\theta (u,v)$ satisfy the inequality of theorem 16.5.5. Contact concentration for model potentials gives these results for model potentials, and extrapolation transfers them to arbitrary potentials of full mass relative to model potentials. As in the preceding section, $\{ \theta \} $ is big.

Proposition 16.5.1. Let $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy

\[ \int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle +\int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle >\int _ X\langle \theta _{\max \{ u,v\} }^ n\rangle . \]

Then $P_\theta (u,v)\ne -\infty $.

Proof. Subtracting the same constant from $u$ and $v$ changes neither the hypothesis nor the conclusion, so we may assume that $u,v\leq 0$. Put $m=\int _ X\langle \theta _{\max \{ u,v\} }^ n\rangle $ and $\phi =P_\theta [\max \{ u,v\} ]$. By monotonicity $\int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle \leq m$ and $\int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle \leq m$, so the strict inequality forces both masses to be positive. Hence $m>0$, and by theorem 16.4.7, $\phi $ is a model potential of mass $m$. Moreover $u,v\leq \max \{ u,v\} \leq \phi $, because the nonpositive potential $\max \{ u,v\} $ is a competitor for $\phi $.

Since $\mu _ u$ and $\mu _ v$ do not charge pole sets, $\mu _ u(\{ u>\phi -k\} )\to \int _ X\langle \theta _ u^ n\rangle $ and $\mu _ v(\{ v>\phi -k\} )\to \int _ X\langle \theta _ v^ n\rangle $ as $k\to \infty $. Choose $k>0$ so large that

\[ \mu _ u(\{ u>\phi -k\} )+\mu _ v(\{ v>\phi -k\} )>m. \]

For $C>k$ put

\[ u_ C=\max \{ u,\phi -C\} ,\quad v_ C=\max \{ v,\phi -C\} ,\quad w_ C=P_\theta (u_ C,v_ C). \]

Since $\phi -C$ is a common minorant, all three potentials have the singularity type of $\phi $ and mass $m$. As $C\to \infty $, $w_ C$ decreases to $P_\theta (u,v)$ by proposition 16.1.6. Suppose that $P_\theta (u,v)=-\infty $, so that $w_ C$ decreases to $-\infty $ identically. Then $\sup _ Xw_ C\to -\infty $ by corollary 14.2.5, and lemma 16.4.2 gives $w_ C\leq \phi -k$ for all large $C$. On $\{ w_ C=u_ C\} $ we then have $u_ C\leq \phi -k$, and similarly for $v_ C$, so corollary 16.2.6 gives

\[ \begin{split} m=\int _ X\langle \theta _{w_ C}^ n\rangle & \leq \mu _{u_ C}(\{ u_ C\leq \phi -k\} ) +\mu _{v_ C}(\{ v_ C\leq \phi -k\} )\\ & =2m-\mu _ u(\{ u>\phi -k\} )-\mu _ v(\{ v>\phi -k\} )<m. \end{split} \]

For the equality we used that, since $C>k$, $u_ C=u$ on the plurifine open set $\{ u>\phi -k\} $, so $\mu _{u_ C}=\mu _ u$ there, and similarly for $v$. This contradiction shows that $P_\theta (u,v)\ne -\infty $. $\square$

Proposition 16.5.2. Let $\phi ,\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $P_\theta [\phi ]=\phi $ and $P_\theta [\psi ]=\psi $, and suppose that $w=P_\theta (\phi ,\psi )\ne -\infty $. Then $P_\theta [w]=w$. Thus the rooftop envelope of two model potentials is a model potential whenever its mass is positive.

Proof. Since $w\leq \phi $, monotonicity gives $P_\theta [w]\leq P_\theta [\phi ]=\phi $, and likewise $P_\theta [w]\leq \psi $.
